=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
THE ARMY AND NAVY CLUB
WASHINGTON 6. D. C.
Marçh 20, 1946.
Ceneral Frank Ross McCoy,
Chairman, Far Eastern Commission,
Washington, D. C.
Dear General McCoy:
While I have not examined the text of the new Japanese constitution, I
have been wondering if its clause purporting henceforth to abolish war as an
instrument of national policy implements such a prohibition with any punitive
provision for Japanese subjects who might violate it.
This aspect would seem to be of the essence. Without appropriate san-
ctions or "teeth," any such proscription might well prove to be only a pious
homily, capable of adroit circumvention in practice. It would afford an acid
test of the bona fides of the professed outlawing of war by the Nipponese. of
course, even the most rigid provision for the punishment of individuals would
be no guarantee against the abuse of power by future meglomaniacs. It neverthe-
less would be a further salutary contribution to the creation of a sorely needed
system of international morality. It must come to be recogmized that a breach
of the international peace is as much, or more, of a crime as disturbing the
peace in municipal law. This is the great objective of the Nurnberg trials, as
0
you know. Their task would be facilitated if there had been more established
public law and precedents applicable to individuals in such cases.
My thought is that study should be given to the inclusion in the new Jap-
anese charter of safeguards which would save it from the crippling defect of
the Kellogg-Briand Pact. The latter merely renounced war, with reservations.
Nc penalities for violations were specified; neither was enforoement machinery
provided. Itseffectiveness naturally was so reduced as to make it of only
nominal value, more moral than real.
The constitutional lawyers may interpose objections to such a clause as
I propose. It would be an innovation, indeed revolutionary. It is inextricably
interwoven with the abridgement of sovereignty, which is the heart of the whole
question of prewenting war. But it should not present insuperable obstacles.
For one thing, the present is an unprecedented opportunity, which may never re-
cur, when Japanese sovereignty is inoperative and that nation is under the com-
plete control of the United Nations, to effectuate such a wholesome principle.
Its application to Japan (and to Germany in due course) could serve as the most
vital milestone in the, if not prevention, then restriction of war.
After defining crimes and fixing punishment, the next matter calling for
careful consideration is the providing of facilities for the trial of Japanese
citisens, including officials, who might offend in this respect. The error,
either by silence or indirection, should be avoided of allowing them to be tried
by their own national courts, which procedure made a farcical miscarriage of
justice of the trials at Leipsig of presumably guilty Germans after World War I.
An excellent solution might well be for any such future culprits to be tried
by the International Court of Justice. This, also, would be promotive of the
prestige and power of the United Nations Organisation, to the support of which

=== Page 17 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
THE ARMY AND NAVY CLUB
WASHINGTON 6. D C
we are pledged.
I do not hold with those persons who claim that the above doctrine is
inharmonious with the duty of military and naval officers to obey orders. It
is just such specious reasoning which has made possible the notorious activities
of the predatory German General Staff and the pathological Shintoism and the
Samurai. A higher standard must be established. The world must come to accept
the Anglo-saxon principle of jurisprudence that law is binding on all individuals,
who must be amenable to it. uch law is illustrated by the aharter of the United
Nations and, let us hope, the revised Japanese constitution.
Heretofore, no sooner would the hostilities of one war cease before the
sand in the hour glass would begin running out anew, with another holocaust
as the end result. That pattern must be broken. The foregoing proposal, while
not a panacea, is another step in that direction, - in man's slow march from
the law of the jungle to that of peace and security under law.
The preceding portion of this letter was drafted several days ago. I
wished to review it before sencing it. For I was aware of the vulnerability
of my proposal. In consequence, I was most interested to read only last night
a thesis put forward by Yalter Lippmann which squares with and offers a means
of implementing the oregoing question of providing sanctions for any individual
Japanese who mirht violate the anti-war clause of their new constitution. Mr.
Lippnann's idea is developed in a forthconing book, "How To Enfore International
Agreenents," a condensation of which is available in "The Reader's Digest" for
April, where 1 saw it.
His plan is ai ned at the devising of means to prevent the improper use of
the atomic bomb, a roal whi ch goes hand in hand with the preceding one of prevent
ing the futare waging of war by the ipponese. Indeed, r. Lippmann postulates
this means as a basis for the prevention of all wars. He, too, is concerned with
the control of iniivi duals, inelu ing officials, upon whom world laws would
directly operate. As he reminds us, "The Uni ted Nations have embraced the prin-
ciple that 'crines are always comaitted by persons' and that 'only sanctions
which reach inivi hals can peicefully and effectively be enforced.in
Dombtless you saw the statesmnlike article of r. Stimson in the arch
issne of arper's agazine" peliog wdth the o  ortunity of fered by the
at de po b to i a rove interrati mal reLations.

Larchmont Gables,
Larchmont, N. Y.
April 16, 1946
Mr. Gordon Bowles,
Division of Cultural Relations,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Bowles:
I hope that this letter finds you in good
health and spirits. I trust that you have had a profitable
trip to Japan.
I am writing you today in the hope that
you have in your possession a copy of the revised constitution
of Japen which is on the agenda of the new Diet in Tokyo.
I am keenly interested in the sections of it that deal with
individual civil rights, and would like to study them care
fully. I shall appreciate it very much, therefore, if you
would send me one.
I have not yet given up my hope of making a
visit to Japen. If there should appear some opportunity,
will you let me know?
Very sincerely yours,
/8/ Toru Matsumoto
/t/Toru Mateumoto

SUBJECT:
Revised Draft of Japanese Constitution

The Honorable
The Sacretary of State
Washington
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to this Mission'e despatches
No. 311, Mrch 19, 1946 and No. 322, March 25, 1946 trane
mitting copies of the official English and Japanese texts of
the Japanese Government's draft constitution, and to enclose
copies in triplicate of the revised draft, written in collo-
quial Japanese end containing a number of minor substantive
changes, made public by the Government April 17. Efforts
to obtain official texts have been unavailing, but it is
believed that the enclosed reproductions printed by the
Asahi (in Japanese) and Nippon Times (in English) of April
18 are accurate in every respect.
In announcing the revised draft to the press, Director
IRIYE Toshiro of the Legislative Bureau stated that collo-
quialization of the new constitution and the use of hire-ne
instead of kata-kana-the form hitherto employed for official
documents-were for the puroose of facilitating study of the
document by the largest possible number of people, before it
was introduced by the special session of the Diet scheduled
for mid-May. He intimated that the text had been based on a
plan submitted by the Society for People's Language, a private
organisation headed by YAMAmOTo Yuzo, well-known novelist and
playwrite, devoted to simplifying the Japanese language. He
stated that the principle of colloquialization will be applied
to all laws and regulations promulgated in future. It was his
view that the draft, now before the Privy Council, would be
aporoved by that body without amendment.
JUL 1 6 1946

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.99)
The substantive changes embodied in the revised draft are
as follows:
Article VII
The list of functions of state performed by the Emperor
on behalf of the people is extended to include "Attestation of
instruments of ratification and other diplomatic documents as
provided for by law".
Article XLIx (Article L in new draft)
Changed to permit convokation of the House of Councillors
during national emergencies when the House of Representatives
has been dissolved and its successor has not yet been elected.
Legislation enacted by the House of Councillors under such cir
cumstances must subsequently receive the aporoval of the Diet.
The Article now reads:
"Then the House of Representetives is ordered diss-
olved, there must be a general election of members of the
House of Representatives within forty (40)days from the
date of dissolution, and the Diet must be convoked within
thirty (30) days from the date of the election. When the
House of Representatives is ordered dissolved, the House
of Councillors must, at the same time, be clomed, except
that the Cabinet may in time of national emergency convoke the
House of Councillors in emergency session.
"Measures enected at such session shall be provisional
and shall become null and void, unless agreed to by the
House of Representatives within a period of ten (1o) days
after the opening of the next session of the Diet.
Article LII (Article LIII in new draft)
Changed to permit secret meetings of either House of the
Diet when desired by two-thirds or more of the members present.
The pertinent clauses of the Article now read:
"Deliberation in each House shall be oublic. However,
a secret meeting may be held where a mejority of two-thirds
or more of those members present passes a resolution therefor.
"Each House shall keep a record of proceedings. This
record shall be published and given general circulation, ex-
cepting such parts of proceedings of secret session as may be
deemed to require secrecy".
Article LXIII
Changed to provide that new Prime Ministers designated by
the House of Representatives shall take office if the House of
Councillors fails to make desimation within 20 days, time in
recess excepted, after the House of Representatives. The
Article now reads:
"The

=== Page 23 === (confidence 0.98)
42
一 3 -
3
"The Prime Minister shall be designated by a resolution
of the Diet. This designation shall precede all other businees.
"If the House of Representetives and the House of Councillors
disagree and if a joint committee of both houses, provided for by
law, cannot reach an agreement, or the House of Councillors fails
to make desi mation within twenty (20) days, exclusive of the
period of recess, after the House of Representatives has made
designation, the decision of the Fouse of Representatives shall
be the decision of the Diet".
Article LXVI
Changed to provide that the retirement age of inferior judges
shall be determined by lam, inestead of being met in the constitution
at 70 years. The Article now rends:
"The judges of the inferior courts shall be appointed
by tre Cabinet from a list of persons nominated by the Suprome
Court. All such judges shall hold office for a term of ton
years with privilege of reaprointment, provided that they shall
be retired upon the attainment of the age us fixed oy law".
Chapter II (Supplementery Provisions)
Four additional Articles dealing with matters of a non-recur
ing nature inserted as follows:
"Article XCVI. This Constitution shall be enforced
as from the day when the period of six months will have elapsed
counting from the day of its promulgation.
"The onforcement of laws necessary for the enforcement
of this Constitution, the election of members of the House of
Councillors and the procedure for the convokation of the Diet
and other preparetory procedures necessary for the enforcement
of this Constitution may be executed before the day prescribed
in the preceding paragraph.
"Article XcvII. As regards those who hold peerage on
the effective dete of this Constitution, their title shall remain
velid for their lives, but no right of peerage shall from thie
time fort! embody within itself eny power of government.
"Article XcvIII. If the House of Councillors is not
constituted before the effective date of this Constitution, the
House of Representetives shall sit as the Diet on that date and
until such time as the House of Councillors shall be constituted.
"Article XcIx. The term of office for half the members
of the Mouse of Councillors serving in the first term under this
Constitution shall be three vears. Members falling under this
category shall be determined in accordance with law".
Press

=== Page 24 === (confidence 0.97)
Press reaction to the colloquialization of the now constitu
has been uniformly favorable. The following views qroted frem
editorial in the Nippon Times of April 20 regarding the subetantive
changes of the revised draft, however, are representative of a
considerable body of informed opinion:
"Although some well conceived improvements have been made,
there are still some s tructural defects, some sections are still
not too logically arrarged, some passages are still too wordy and
redundant, some provisions are still rether fragmentary and in-
adequate while other provisions are still too obviously of topical
interest and do not heve the lasting and univereal quality which
the provisions of a permanent constitution should possess."
The same editorial states:
"Even with the heightened interest in politics during the
recent electoral campaign the candiastes and the voters remained
altogether too ap:thetic to the vital problem of the constitution.
while working themselves into excitement over far less important
issues".
It is regretted that efforts to obtain the official texts of
the revised draft constitution and shortage of clerical help have
caused delay in transmission.
Respectfully yours,
Georre Atcheson, Jr.

approved the paper, I would have to reserve my position
on it. After a lengthy disoussion on paragraph 2(b)
as a result of a proposed amendment by the Soviet mem-
ber, the Committee voted to delete the entire paragraph.
The Committee then considered ohanges suggested in
8
paragraph 2(o) by the United States member of the Steer-
6
ing Committee. After further disoussiom the Committee
voted to delete all of paragraph 2 but adjourned before
agreement could be reaohed as to a possible substitute
0
for paragraph 2. The United Kimgdom member suggested
11
the paper consist of paragraph one only, which the

9
such a paper would be too vague. Most members of the
3
Committee, however, favored a statement to the effeot
that the present Diet should not be permitted to appreve
the new Constitution. The Committee 1s to meet Monday
afternoon, May 6th to consider an alternative for para-
graph 2.
The New Zealand representative presented the follow-
ing proposal which was tentatively supported by the United
States member:
"It will not be in accord with the principles
of paragraph l(a) and 1(c) 1f the new Constitution
1s either considered or adopted solely by the
present Diet."
The French member suggested the following:
"Disposition should be taken to ensure that
any new constitution will, after a year of trial,
be conrirmed expressly by the electorate in an ap-
Dropriate manner."

APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. On 7 March 1946 General MacArthur made a publio
announcement which read in part as follows:
"It is with a sense of deep satisfaction that I am
today able to announce a decision of the Emperor and
Government of Japan to submit to the Japanese people a
new and enlightened Constitution which has my fuli ap-
proval. This instrument has been drafted after pains-
tal:ing investigation and frequent conference between
members of the Japanese Government and this headquarters
following my initial directive to the Cabinet five months
ago."
2. The Preamble to this draft Constitution reads in part
as follows:
"We, the Japanese people, acting through our duly
elected representatives in the National Diet, determined
that we shall secure for ourselves and our posterity the
fruits of peaceful cooperation with all nations and the
blessings of liberty throughout the land, and resolved
that never again shall we be visited with the horrors of
war, through the action of government, do proclaim the
sovereignty of the people's will and do ordain and estab-
l1sh this Constitution founded upon the universal prin-
ciple that government is a sacred trust, the authority
for which is derived from the people, the powers of which
are exercised by the representatives of the people and
the benefits of which are enjoyed by the people and we
reject and revoke all constitutions, laws, ordinanoes
and rescripts in conflict herewith.
3. On March 12, 1946 Secretary Byrnes was asked 1f the
proposed Japanese Constitution had been submitted for approval
to the other major Allied powers before publication. He
replied that according to his information 1t had not been sub-
mitted and stated that the Japanese Constitution had been drawn
up by the Japanese Government under its right to do so. He
further stated that "before the Constitution beoomes oonsti-
tutionally effective it will in some way or other oome before
the Far Eastern Commission."
4. The Far Eastern Commission adopted a polioy decision
on March 20, which was tranamitted by the United States Govern-
ment as a directive to the Supreme Commander on the

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.90)
54
TOP SECRET
of a Japanese Constitution. This directive stated in part
follows: "The opening sentences of this draft proposed ar
Constitution of March 7, 19467 1ndicate to the Commission that
it will be presented to the first session of the Japanese Diet
which will be chosen at the forthcoming general eleotions. The
Conmission therefore assumes that this and possibly other texts
will be debated in the Diet and that amendments may be offered
and perhaps other proposals introduced." "....The Commission
desires that the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers make
clear to the Japanese Government that the Far Eastern Comais
sion must be given an opportunity to pass upon the final draft
of the Constitution to determine whether it 1s consistent with
the Potsda: Declaration and any other controlling document
before it 1s finally approved by the Diet and becomes legally
valid.
"The Commission believes that in this way hasty aotion
by the Japanese Diet will be prevented and time given for all
elements inside and outside the Diet to consider tils very im-
portant question and to brin to that consideration all avail-
able tou  t produced by the freely expressed will of the
Japeerople.

8. At 1ts eleventh meeting on May 13, 1946, the Far

mittcd to SCAP:
"The criteria for the adoption of a new constitution
should be such as to ensure that the constitution when
finally adopted, is, in fact, a free expression of the
will of the Japanese people. It 1s recommended that, to
this end, these three principles should be observed:
"a. Adequate time and opportunity should be allowed
for the full discussion and consideration of the terms
of a new constitution.
"b. Complete legal continuity from the constitution
of 1889 to the new constitution should be assured.
"c. The new constitution should be adopted in suoh
a manner as to denonstrate that it will affirmatively
express the free will of the Japanese people."

APPENDIX "C"
Proposed Statement by United States Representative 
The Far Eastern Commission
on
The Function of the Commission in Passing
on the New Japanese Constitution
1. The Japanese Government and people are competent to
adopt c new Constitution provided it is approved in accordance
with Japanese constitutional requirements and is consistent
with the provisions of the Terms of Surrender, including the
Potsdan Declaration, and of other controlling documents.
The Potsdam Declaration contains the following statements
which describe certain features which must characterize a new
Japanese Constitution: (1) "The Japanese Government shall
remove all obstacles to the revival and strengthening of
democratic tendencies among the Japanese people. Freedom of
speech, of religion and of thought, as well as respect for the
fundcnental rights, shall be established"; (2) "The occupying
forces of the Allies shall be withdrawn from Japan as soon as
these objoctives have been accomplished and there has been
established, in accordance with the freely expressed will of
the Japanese people a peacefully inclined and responsible
Governnent".
2. The Far Eastern Commission has the function "to
formulate the policies, principles, and standards in conformity
with which the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under
the Terms of Surrender mcy be acconplished".
Tho Far Eastern Commission, therefore, has the authority
to detornine the principles and standards in accordance with
which the new Japanese Constitution should fulrill the pro-
visions of the Potsdam Declaration.

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
2
TOP SEORET
3. The Far Eastern Commission has the right to pass
a new Constitution to determine whether it is consistent with
the provisions of the Potsdam Declaration and of pertinent
policy decisions of the Far Eastern Conmission.
The Secretary of State, on March 12, 1946, stated that
"before the Constitution becomes constitutionally effective it
will in some way or other come before the Far Eastern Commission".
The United States representative on the Far Eastern Commission
joined with the other representatives on the Commission in voting
for the following provision: "The Commission desires that the
Supreme Conmmander for the Allied Powers make clear to the Japanese
Government that the Far Eastern Commission must be given an
opportunity to pass upon the final draft of the Constitution
to determine whether it is consistent with the Potsdam Declar-
ation and any other controlling document before it is finally
approved by the Diet and becomes legally valid".
4. The competence of the Far Eastern Connission "to pass
upon the final draft of the Constitution" is linited to deter-
mining whether the Constitution is consistent with the Potsdan
Declaration and any other controlling documents. The Conmission
appears to have been given no authority to "pass upon" the draft
Constitution in any other respect or for any other purpose.
5. Since the Japanese Government and people are compotent
to adopt a new Constitution provided it is not determined to be
inconsistont with certain provisions prescribed by the Allied
Powers, a draft Constitution will in due course become effective,
unless the Far Eastern Commission finds that it is not con-
sistent with the Potsdam Declaration and other requirements.
There is no provision for a formal approval of the Constitution
by the Far Eastern Comnission. In fact, acceptance, amenament
or rejection by the Far Eastern Conmission of each section or
paragraph would itself violate the intention of the Potsdam

=== Page 58 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SBORET
Declaration that the new government in Japan must be
"in accordance with the freely expressed will of the 
people".
6. When the Far Eastern Cormission passes on the draft
Japanese Constitution, the Commission may conclude that it 1s
not consistent with the Potsdan Declaration and other require-
ments and may insist on adequate changes in the draft. If the
Commission does not find that the draft is inconsistent with
those requirements, the draft Constitution, in cccordance with
the conipetonce of the Japanese Government and people to adopt
a new Constitution, and without fornal action by the Conmission,
will becone effective after it has fulfilled Japanese cone-
titutional provisions.
7. It would be advisable for the Far Eastern Commission
to pass upon the Constitution just before rather than just
after it has been adopted by the Diet. This is the view of the
Far Eastern Cormission, as expressed in its policy decision of
March 20. The best time night be after the Cormittee of the
Wholc, if the Diet has this procedure, has passed it, but before
the full Diet has voted on it.

unchanged when finally adopted, the instrument will
permit development in Japan of a democratic state fully
conforming to existing Allied policy."
On ay ll the Far Eastern Commission approved a
set of criteria for adopting a constitution, one of
which was that "adequate time and opportunity should
be allowed for the full discussion and consideration
of the terms of a new constitution". These criteria
were sent to acArthur in the form of a directive.
In perso nal communication with r. Peterson and
General LcCoy, General MacArthur has indicated that
"speed is of the essence" in the matter of adopting
the Japanese constitution, but he has also said that
it will probably require several months debate in the
Diet

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Diet before adoption. Actually, the Japanese Diet could
adopt the constitution in the matter of a few days. It
seems highly improbable that it will. But General MaoArthur
has issued no order requiring full debate and has given
the FEC no olear indication of his attitude toward the
directives issued him in this matter.
If you are called upon to give Dr. Evatt a reply
I would suggest that you inform him that General MaoArthur
has directives, based on FEC policy decisions, oalling
for adequate consideration of the terms of the new con-
stitution; and that it must be assumed he will aot in
accordance with those directives in assuring adequate
consideration in the Diet of the draft constitution.
Related to the adoption of the constitution is the
FEC position that the Commission must pass on any new
Japanese constitution to determine whether the consti-
tution is in conformity with the general objectives set
forth in the Potsdam Deolaration and the Terms of Surrender.
General Macarthur, while taking issue with this position
of the FEC on grounds that it is inconsistent with the
proviso that the constitution represent the freely expressed
will of the Japanese people, has indicated a willingness
to comply with the FEC decision.
SWNCC has prepared a paper which contains a polioy
statement to be made by General McCoy to the Commission
setting forth the American attitude on "The Function of
the Commission in Passing on the New Japanese Constitution."
This statement will go into some detail on various phases
of the constitutional issue. A copy will be sent to
General wacarthur for his guidance. It was originally
intended that the President would write a letter to
General wacArthur setting forth our position. This idea
has been dropped on grounds that it was "undignified" for
the President to ∞mmunicate "advice" to MacArthur and
that it would be improper for the President to "instruct"
him because in his position as SCAP he is properly subjodt
to direction by the FEC through the medium of directives
transmitted by this Governnent. It was therefore decided
that the statement of policy should go to General MoCoy
with a copy to General wacArthur in the "hope" that it
would have the desired effeot.
JV.

894.001/7-346

by the Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall
prepare directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the
Commission and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through
the appropriate United States Government agency."
At the eichteenth meeting of the Far Eastern Commission
held at 2516 Massachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D.C., on
2 July 1946, the enclosed policy decision relative to Basic Principles
For A New Japanese Constitution, was unanimously approved.
As Socretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have
been instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the
Commission, in order that the appropriate directives may be prepared
and transmitted to the Su rene Commander in accordance with the
Terns of Reference.

ENCLOSURE
BASIC PRINCIPLES FOR A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
1.
The Japanese Constitution should recognize that soveroign
power resides in the people. It should be so framed as to provide
for:
a.
A representative government based upon universal adult
suffrage consisting of:
(1) An executive deriving its authority from and
responsible to either the electorate or a fully
representative legislative body;
(2) A legislature, fully representative of the
electorate, which should have full legislative
powers, including full control over raising of
public revenue and expenditure of public funds;
b.
The •stablishment of an independent judiciary;
0.
The guarantee of fundamental civil rights to all Japan-
ese and to all persons within Japanese jurisdiction. All
Japanese shall enjoy equal rights before the law and no special
privileges of particular social groups such as the nobility
shall be allowed;
d.
The popular election of heads of institutions of local
government such as prefectures, cities, towns and villages;
e.
The popular election of local assemblies such as pre-
fectural, city, town, and village.
f.
The adoption of constitutional amendments in a manner
which will give effect to the freely expressed will of the
Japanese people.
2.
Though the ultimate form of gevernment in Japan is to be
estabiished by the freely expressed will of the Japanese people, the
retention of the Emperor Institution in its present constitutional
form is not considered consistent with the foreroing general objeotives.
Consequently, the Japanese shoule be eneourared to abolish the Emperor
Institution or to refora it alonr more demoeratie lines.

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.98)
3. If the Japanese people decide that the Emperor Institution
is not to be retained, constitutional safeguards against the institution
will obviously not be required, but the constitution will have to oonform
to the requirements of paragraph 1 and shall also provide:
a.
That the legislature shall have sole authority over
financial measures and any other organ shall possess only a
temporary veto power over other legislative measures;
That the prime minister and the ministers of state,
all of whom shall be civilians and of whom a majority,
including the prime minister, shall be selected from the
Diet, shall form a Cabinet colleotively responsible to
the legislature. If a system of government is adopted
whereby the chief executive is elected to that office by
the people, the provision that a majority of the Cabinet
members shall be chosen from the legislature should not
necessarily apply;
0.
That the legislative organ shall have the power to
meet at will.
4. If the Japanese decide to retain the Institution of the
Emperor, the following safeguards in addition to those enumerated in
1 and 3 above will be necessary:
a.
When a Cabinet loses the confidence of the legislature it
shall either resign or appeal to the electorate;
b. The Emperor shall have no powers other then those to be
conferred on him by the new Constitution. He shall aot in all
cases in accordance with the advice of the Cabinet;
C.
The Emperor shall be deprived of ell military authority
such as that provided in Articles XI, XII, XIII, and XIV of
Chapter 1 of the Constitution of 1889;
d.
All property of the Imperial household shall be declared
property of the State. The expenses of the Imperial household shall
be appropriated by the legislature.
5.
The retention of the Privy Council and the House of Peers
in their present form and with their present powers is not considered
consistent with the foregoing general objectives.

=== Page 93 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to Despatch No. 495, dated July 9, 1946, from
the United States Politisal Adviser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
subject "Draft Constitution Proposed by the Japan Commumist Party."
NIPPON TIMES, July 2, 1946
DRAFT ONSTITUTION PUBLISHED BY REDS
Advocate Republican Form of State and Abolition of
Emperor System
The Japan Communist Party on Sunday published its draft constitu-
tion advocating the abolition of the Emperor system and the establish-
ment of a republican form of Government.
In mmking the draft Constitution public, the Communist Party in a
statement said that it wished to submit its draft constitution to public
discussion prior to submitting it to the Diet.
In its preamble, the draft constitution of the Communists described
the Emperor system as "incompatible with the maintenance of freedom and
happiness of the Japanese peoples and declared that only through the
adoption of a republican form of Government will "the Japanese people be
able to create a society where there will be no room for exploitations".
The draft constitution, however, made no reference of the controversial
issue regarding the renunciation of war.
Article one of the draft constitution, which is titled "The Con-
stitution of the Japanese People's Republic," places the sovereignty of
the state into the hands of the people. Other salient points of the
draft constitution included the following:
(1) All persons shall be equal before the law of the state and
shall possess all fundamental human rights guaranteed under the consti-
tution.
(2) The Imperial Family system and the Peers system shall be
abolished.
(3) Women shall have equal rights as men in every field of life.
(4) Laborers shall be guaranteed by the state to have the right
to own a home, receive education and an appropriate vacation.
(5) All persons over 18 years of age shall have the right to vote
and he elected. Elections will be held under a proportional representa-
tive system, and the Diet shall consist of one house.
(6) The Diet shall supervise and guard the sovereignty of the
people.
(7) The Diet shall elect a 25-men committee whose chairman shall
represent the republic.
(8) All judges shall be elected by the Diet.
(9) Local governments shall be established on a self-governmenting ba
(lo) Police and all other public officials shall be elected.

DIRECTIVE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
BASIC PRINCIPLES FOR A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
The following statement of policy, edopted by the Far
Eastern Commission on 2 July 1946 under the provisions of para-
graph II, A, one, of its terms of reference has been received
from the State, War and Navy Departments for transmission to
you as a directive for your guidance in accordance with para-
groph III, one, of those terms of reference. You should take
ppropriate steps consistent with the directives on Draft
Constitution for Japan* and Principles Governing the Machinery
for the Adoption of a New Japanese Constitution,** to insure
that any constitution adopted by the Japanese conforms to the
principles set forth in this statement:
"1. The Japanese Constitution should recognize that
sovereign power resides in the people. It should be so
framed as to provide for:
. A representative government based upon universal
adult suffrage consisting of:
(1) An executive deriving its authority from and
responsible to either the electorate or a fully
* Serial No. 36
** Serial No. 47

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
representative legislative body;
(2) A legislature, fully representative
electorate, which should have full legislative
and expenditure of public funds;
b. The establishment of an independent judiciary;
c. The guarantee of fundamental civil rights to all
Japanese and to all persons within Japanese jurisdiotion.
All Japanese shall enjoy equal rights before the law and
no special privileges of particular social groups such as
the nobility shall be allowed;
d. The popular election of heads of institutions of
local government such as prefectures, cities, towns and
villages;
e. The popular election of local assemblies such as
prefecturel, city, town, and village;
f. The adoption of constitutional amendments in a
manner which will give effect to the freely expressed will
of the Japanese people.
2. Though the ultimate form of government in Japan is to
be esteblished by the freely expressed will of the Japanese
people, the retention of the Emperor Institution in its
present constitutional form is not considered consistent with
the foregoing general objectives. Consequently, the Japanese
should be encouraged to abolish the Emperor Institution or
to reform it along more democratic lines.
3. If the Japanese people decide that the Emperor In-
stitution is not to be retained, constitutional safeguards
against the institution will obviously not be required, but
the Constitution will have to conform to the requirements
of paragraph 1 and shall also provide:
a. That the legislature shall have sole authority over
a temporary veto power over other legisletive

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)
b. That the Prime Minister and the Ministers of
all of whom shall be civilians and of whom a majority
Diet, shall form a Cabinet collectively responsible to
the legislature. If a system of government is adopted
whereby the Chief Executive is elected to that office by
the people, the provision that a majority of the Cabinet
members shall be chosen from the legislature should not
necessorily apply;
c. That the legislative organ shall have the power to
meet at will.
4. If the Japanese decide to retain the Institution of the
Emperor, the following safeguards in addition to those enum-
erated in 1 and 3 above will be necessary;
a. When a Cabinet loses the confidence of the legis-
lature it shall either resign or appeal to the electorate;
b. The Emperor shall have no powers other than those
to be conferr3d on him by the new Constitution. He shall
act in all cases in accordence with the edvice of the
Cabinet;
c. The Emperor shall be deprived of all military
authority such as thet provided in Articles XI, XII, XIII,
end XIv of Chapter 1 of the Constitution of 1889;
d. All property of the Imperial household shall be
declared property of the State. The expenses of the Imper-
ial household shall be appropriated by the legislature.
5. The retention of the Privy Council and the House of
Peers in their present form and with their present powers
is not considered consistent with the foregoing generel
objectives."

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)

SOVIET STATEENT
on the
AUTHORITY OF THE FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
TO APPROVE A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
The paper FEC-O31/23, wnich is submitted for our consideration
today, in its original form contained two paragraphs and out of these
the first paragraph covered the provisions for the review of a new
Japanese constitution while the seoond one dealt with the provisions
for its going into effect. Now we are offerred to adopt only the
first paragraph of that document concerning the provisions for the
review of tie constitution.
I wish to state here that the Soviet Delegation, taking into
account some of the considerations wnich have been expressed here,
is prepared to concede witn respect to the last sentence of the
first paragraph and accept the wording which was suggested by the
U. S. Delegation. But I am unable to consent upon the exclusion from
that document of its second paragraph. It is my view that the question
of provisions for the constitution's going into effect cannot be divorced
from the question of reviewing it, and logically it should inevitably
precede it. Tnere is not much sense in speaking of now and when and
who is going to review the constitution unless we come to a preliminary
agreament on how and when it goes into effect.
At the last meeting of the Commission the Soviet Delegation pro-
posed to make an amendment in the original second paragraph to the effect
that the new Japanese constitution prior to its going into effect should

=== Page 125 === (confidence 0.99)
103
come for approval by the Far Rastern Commission.
This suggestion of the Soviet Delegation was contended by some
of the members of the Far Eastern Commission. The Sovist Delegation
considers it necessary to give a complete clarity to this question
as it is of fundamental importance and upon its solution it will be
dependent whether the Far Eastern Commission intends to live up to
the spirit and letter of the Moscow decisions of the Three Ministers
in respect to the scope of its jurisdiction.
In the Soviet Government's view it is doubtless that such question
as the new Japanese constitution's going into effect does fall under
the provisions of the Article 3, Part III of the Moscow decisions,
wnich provides that substantial changes in the Japanese constitutional
structure are subject to agreement within the Far Eastern Commission.
This question is one of those upon which the U. S. Government should
necessarily issue a directive to the Supreme Commander after an appropriate
consultation with the Far Eastern Commission and after reaching in the
Commission an agreement on acceptability of the Japanese constitution
from the viewpoint of application of the principles of the Potsdam
Declaration. After this only, the Japanese constitution approved as
complying with the principles of the Potsdam Declaration can go into
effect. A different approach to the question would be contrary to the
Moscow decision re arding the terms of competence of the Far Eastern
Commission.
In stating this point of view and bearing in mind the extreme im-
portance of this question, the Soviet Delegation invites all the repre-
sentatives of the member-nations of the Far Eastern Commission to define
-2

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.98)
104
their position on this question and state formally their viewpoint
regarding the Far Eastern Commission's prerogative to approve, in
accordnce with the Moscow decisions of the Three Ministers, the new
Japanese con:ticution as the necessary prerequisite of the latter's
oin into eifect.

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.98)
1.5
POHEJHINELAL
UNITED STATES POLICY IN REGARD TO THE AUTHORITY
OF THE FAR EASTERN COMMISSION TO APPROVE
A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
1. The Japanese Government and people are competent to adopt a
new Constitution provided it is cnacted in accordance with Japanese
constitutional requirements and is consistent with the provisions of
the Terms of Surrender, including the Fotsdam Declaration and of the
policy decisicns of the Far Eastern Commission.
The Potsdam Declaration contains the following statements which
describe certain features wnich must characterise a new Japanese Con-
stitution: (1) "The Japanese Government shall remove all obstacles to
the revival and strengthening of democratic tendencies among the Jape-
nese people. Freedom of speech, of religion and of thought, as well
as respect for the fundamental rights, shall be established"; (2) The
occupying forces of the Allies shall be withdrawn from Japan as soon as
these objectives have been accomplished and there has been established,
in accordance with the freely expressed will of the Japanese people a
peacefully inclined and responsible Goverment".
2. The Far Eastern Commission has 'the function "to formulate the
policies, principles, and standards in conformity with which the fulfill-
ment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender may be ac-
complished".
The Far Eastern Commission, therefore, has the autnority to deter-
mine the principles and standards in accordance with which the new
Japanese Constitution should fulfill the provisions of the Potsdam De-
claration.
The Far Eastern
COMFIDENTIAL

=== Page 128 === (confidence 0.98)
100
2
The Far Eastern Commission determined these principles and
standards by a policy decision, FEC 031/19, adopted on July 2, 1946.
3. The Far Rastern Commission has the right to pass upon a
new Constitution to determine whether it is consistent with the pro-
visions of the Potsdam Declaration and of pertinent policy decisions
of the Far Eastern Comnission.
The Secretary of State, on March 12, 1946, stated that "before
the Constitution becomes constitutionally effective it will in same
way or other come before the Far Eastern Commission". The United
States representative on the Far Eastern Commission joined with the
other representatives on the Commission in voting for the following
provision: "The Commission desires that the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers make clear to the Japanese Government that the Far
Eastern Commission must be given an opportunity to pass upon the
final draft of the Constitution to determine whether it is consistent
with the Potsdam Declaration and other controlling documents before
it is finally approved by the Diet and becomes legally valid."
4. The competence of the Far Eastern Commission "to pass upon
the final draft of the Constitution" is limited to determining whether
the Constitution is consistent with the Potsdam Declaration and other
controlling documents, including the policy decisions of the Commis-
sion. The Commission appears to have been given no authority to
"pass upon" the draft Constitution in any other respect or for any
other purpose.
5. The Terms
CORFEDEMPIAL

=== Page 129 === (confidence 0.99)
107
-3-
5. The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission and
of the Allied Council contain no provision, either expressed or im-
plied, that a new Japanese Constitution requires the approval of the
Far Eastern Commission.
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission state
(III,3):
"The U. S. Government may issue interim directives
to the Supreme Commander....provided that any direotives
dealing with fundamental changes in the Japanese consti-
tutional structure....will be issued only following con-
sultation and following the attainment of agreement in
the Far Eastern Commission."
This requirement bars the United States Government from issuing an
interim directive dealing with fundamental changes in the Japanese con-
stitutional structure, and provides that any directive dealing with the
subject must follow the attainment of agreement in the Far Bastern Com-
mission. The recent directive to SCAP containing the "Basic Principles
for a New Japanese Constitution" (FBC O3l/19), a policy decision adopted
by the Far Eastern Commission on July 2, 1946, was sent in accordance
with this section of the Terms of Reference. There is nothing in this
section, however, which requires that a directive must be issued to valid
ate an action of the Japanese Government, such as the adoption of a new
Constitution, which is within the competence of that Government pro-
vided the action is not inconsistent with existing requirements of the
Allied Powers.
The Terms
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.97)
108
-4
The Terms of Reference of the Allied Council state (section 6):
"If, regarding the implementation of policy decisions of
the Far Rastern Commission on questions concerning....fundamental
changes in the Japanese constitutional structure....a mamber of
the Council disagrees with the Supreme Commander (or his Deputy),
the Supreme Commander will withhold the issuance of orders on
these questions pending agreement thereon in the Far Rastern Com-
mission".
This requirement is applicable solely to instances when the Far
Eastern Commission has issued a policy decision concerning a fundamental
change in the Japanese constitutional structure. It does not require
the Far Eastern Commission to issue any policy decision on the subject.
It therefore does not stipulate or imply that a new Japanese Constitu-
tion must receive the approval of the Far Rastern Commission.
6. Since the Japanese Government and people are competent to adopt
a new Constitution provided it is not inconsistent with the provisions
preseribed by the Allied Powers, a draft Constitution will in due course
become effective, unless the Far Bastern Commission finds that it is not
consistent with the Potsdam Declaration and other requirements. A formal
approval of the Constitution by the Far Kastern Commission in the sense of
acceptance, amendment or rejection by the Commission of each section or
paragraph would violate the intention of the Potsdam Declaration that the
new government in Japan must be established "in accordance with the freely
expressed will of the Japanese people".

In order that the Japanese people may have an oppors

the continuing jurisdiction of the Far Eastern Commission at any.

this same period. The Far Eastern Commission, in determining
whether the Japanese Constitution is an expression of the free
will of the Japanese people, may require a referendum or some
other appropriate procedure for ascertaining Japanese opinion with
respect to the Constitution.

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.98)
ENOLOSURE
UNITED STATES POLICY IN REGARD TO THE
ADOPTION OF A NEN JAPANEBE CONSTITUTAS
1. The Japanese Government and people are oompetent to
adopt a new Constitution provided it is enaoted in ocordance
with Japanese ccn:ilutional requirements and is consistent
with tile provisions of the Terms of Surrender; inoluding the
Potsdam Deolaration and of the policy decisions of the Far
Eastern Commission.
The Potsdam Declaration contcins the following state
ments which describe certain features which must charaoterize a
new Japanese Constitution: (1) "The Japanese Government shall
remove all obatacles to the revival and strengthening of demo-
cratic tendencies among the Japanese people. Freedom of speech,
of religion and of thought, as well as respeir for the funda
mental rights, shall be established"; (2: "The occupying foroes
of the Allies shall be withdrawn from Japan as soon as these
objectives have been accomplished end there has been established,
in accordance with the freely expressed will of the Japanese
people a peacefully inclined and responsible Government".
2. The Far Eastern Commission has the funotion "to formu-
late the policies, principles, and standards in conformity with
which the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the
Terms of Surrender may be accomplished".
The Far Eastern Commission, therefore, has the authority
to determine the principles and standards in accordance with
which the new Japanese Constitution should fulrill the provisions
of the Potsdam Declaration.
The Far Eastern Commission determined these prinoiples
and standards by a policy decision, FEC O31/19, adopted on July
2, 1946.
3. The Far Eastern Commission has the right to pass
new Constitution to determine whether it is

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.98)
 TOP 3ECRET
provisions of the Potsdam Declaration and of pertinent pola
decisions of the Far Eastern Commission.
The Secretary of State, on March 12, 1946, stated th
"before the Constitution becomes constitutionally effeotive it
will in some way or other come before the Far Eastern Commi
slon". The Unit States representative on the Far Eastern
Commission j j vi:n the other representatives on the Comai
sion in votigor he following provision: "The Commission
desires tht t Surrcme Commander for the Allied Powers make
clear to the opo ose Government that the Far Eastern Commis-
sion must be given an opportunity to pass upon the rinal draft
of the Constitution to determine whetier it is consistent with
the Potcdan Declaration and other controlling doouments before
it is finally approved by the Diet and becomts legally valid.
4. The competence of the Far Eastern Comanasion "to pass
upon the finel draft of the Constitution" is liaited to deter
mining whether the Constitution is consistent with the Potsdam
Declaration and other controlling documents, including the
policy decisions of the Commission. The Commission appears to
have been civen no authority to "pass upon" the draft Constitu-
tion in any other respect or for any other purpose.
5. The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Comnission
and of the Allied Council contain no provision, either expressed
or implied, that a new Japanese Constitution requires the ap-
proval of the Far Eastern Commission.
The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission
state (III,3) :
"The U.S. Government may issue interim direotives to
the Supreme Commander....provided that any direotives
dealing with fundamental changes in the Japanese consti-
tutional structure....will be issued only following con-
sultation and following the attainment of agreement in
the Far Eastern Commission.

=== Page 142 === (confidence 0.96)
thée regussement bere the Tase
tesuing an interim disective dealing wim fum
the Japanese constitutional structure, and previe
direotive dealing with the subjeot must fellew to
of agreement in the Far Eastern Commission. The eeem
Constitution" (FEC O31/19), a policy decision adopte  
Far Eastern Commission on July 2, 1946, was sent in aocera
with this section of the Terms of Reference. There 1s nothing
in this section, however, which requires that a direetive must
be issued to validate an action of the Japanese Government, such
as the adoption of a new Conetitution, which 1s within the com-
petence of that Government provided the action is not inoonaist-
ent with existing requirements of the Allied Powers.
The Terms of Reference of the Allied Counoil state
(section 6):
"If, regarding the implelentation of policy decisions
of the Far Eastern Conmission on questions conoerning.....
fundamental changes in the Japanese constitutional
structure....a member of the Council disagrees with the
Supreme Commander (or his Deputy), the Supreme Commander
will withhold the issuance of orders on these questions.
pending agreement thereon in the Far Eastern Commission.
This requirement 1s applicable solely to instanoes when
the Far Ecstern Commission has issued a policy decision conoern-
ing a fundanental change in the Japanese constitutional struo-
ture. It does not require the Far Eastern Commission to issue
any policy decision on the subject. It therefore does not
stipulate or imply that a new Japanese Constitution must receivo
the approval of the Far Eastern Commission.
6. Since the Japanese Government and people re cogetent
to adopt a new Constitution provided it is not ineonsiste 
the provisions presoribed by the Allied Povers, a arert
tution will in due course become effeetive, unlese
Eastern Gommission rinés that i3 ls no

=== Page 143 === (confidence 0.98)
，TOPSEORT
Potsdam Doclaration and other requirements. A formal
of the Constitution by the Far Eastern Commission in the momm
of acceptance, amendment or rejection by the Commission of each
section or paragraph would violate the intention of the Potsdam
Declaration that the new government in Japan must be established
"in accordance with the freely expressed will of the Japanese
people".
7. When the Far Eastern Commission passes on the draft
Japanese Constitution, the Commission may conclude that it is
not consistent with the Potsdan Declaration and other require
ments and nay insist on adequate changes in the draft. If the
Commission does not find that the draft 1s inconsistent with
these requirements, the Draft Constitution, 1n accordance with
the conpetence of the Japanese Governnent and people to adopt a
new Constitution, and without fornal action by the Commission,
will becone effective after it has fulfilled Japanese constitu-
tional provision.

MESSAGE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POM
CONSULTATION WITH THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE
ALLIED POWERS ON THE TEXT OF THE CONSTITUTION
The following communication from the Far Eastern
Commission, forwarded through its chairman under the provisions
of paragraph VI, 1, of its terms of reference, is transmitted
to you at the request of the State, Var and Navy Departments:
"The Far Eastern Commission requests the chairman of
the Commission to arrange for consultation between the
Commission and the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
regarding the fulfillment by the draft of the new Japanese
constitution of the principles contained in the Potsdam
Declaration end in the Commission's policy decision concerning
the constitution, FEC O3l/19.* To facilitate this consul-
tation the Commission requests the chairman to convey to the
Supreme Commander the substence of the following communication
and to state to him that the Commission would greatly appre-
cinte his views on the questions raised therein:
1. The Far Eastern Commission is making a preliminary
study of the draft of the new Japanese constitution, which
is now before the Diet, to determine whether the present
* Serial Nc. 54.

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
dreft satisfies the oriteris este
in its statement of polioy on basic prt
Potsdam Declaretion and other controlling
comments which the Supreme Commander may wish to
the points raised in this preliminary study would
helpful to the Commission's work.
2. In the course of this preliminary study doubts 
arisen as to whether in certain respects the draft con-
stitution satisfies the criteria established by the Com-
mission in its stetement of policy on basic prinoiples
(FEC 031/19). The following examples may be cited:
(a). Cabinet. FEC O31/19 provides that a majority
of the Ministers of State, including the Prime Minister
shall be selected from the Diet. The draft constitutio
does not now provide for this principle of seleotion.
(b). Suffrage. FEC O31/19 provides thet the Japanes
Government be 'based upon universal adult suffrage.'
It appears doubtful whether the draft constitution
makes adequate provision in this respect. While Artiol
39 states that 'both Houses shall consist of elooted
members, representative of all the people' it is a
question whether this provision is sufficiently speoifio
to fulfill the requirement of FEC O31/19. Furthermore,
it might be point out that, though it is provided in
Article 40 of the draft constitution that in respeot
of the qualifications of electors for both Houses there
should be no discrimination because of race, creed, ,
social status, or family origin, the door is apparently
left open for discrimination on such grounds as a,
education, property, or income.
(c). Sovereignty. FEC O31/19 provides that 
constitution should recognize that sovereign
resides in the people.' The Commission ts
whether the draft constitution fulfill

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)
and has been confronted with a
The draft constitution provides in the
the Japanese people ... do proolaim the
the people's will.' Aside from the question as to
whether this clause adequately meets the provisios 
FEC O31/19, legel opinion has been presented to the
Commission to the effect that a preamble is merely
declaratory and explanatory statement, the funotion of
which is not to confer power and whose provisions are
not a source of obligation. The United States Supreme
Court has so held with respect to the preamble of the
United Stetes Constitution. The legal position in Japan
is more obscure, as it is impossible to draw a satis-
factory conclusion on the basis of the preamble of the
Meiji constitution because it has had the force of an
imperial edict.
Article 1 also contains a reference to sovereignty.
It resds: 'The Emperor shsll be the symbol of the State
and of the unity of the people, deriving his position
from the sovereign will of the people.' It is doubtful
whether this article, which deals with the status of
the Emperor, adequately fulfills the provisions in the
basic principles regarding sovereignty.
To avoid all doubts on both points, it has been
suggested that it should be clearly stated in the body
of the constitution that 'Sovereign power resides in
the people.' The Commission would value the Supreme
Commander's view.
3. The further question arises as to whether the draft
constitution accords with the Potsdam Declaration.
(a). The draft constitution leaves several importam
matters to be enacted in subsequent laws, a situa
which mekes it difficult to determine the full 
cation of the articles concerned.

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
Imperial House Law (Articles 2 and 5)
and methods of election of the two Mouses
and the joint committees of both Houses
57 and 63). The Commission realises that
matters may properly be left for detailed
by subsequent legislation. It queries, however,
it would not better accord with the Potsdam Declarati
to include in the constitution the basic provisions
governing some of these matters, such as the compositio
of the House of Councilors.
(b). The Commission would raise the query whether
Article 54 of the draft constitution should not set out
clearly the grounds on which a member of the Diet may
be expelled, in order to prevent this article from
being used to exclude representatives of minority
groups.
(c). In regard to Article 63, the Commission queries
whether the selection of a Prime Minister should not be
a matter for the House of Representatives alone, rather
then for both Houses. This procedure would acoord with
practice in other democratic governments where the
executive is responsible to the legislature, and would
seem to follow naturally from Article 65."

FAR EASTERN COMMISSION
2516 MASSACHUSETTS AVENUE. N. W.
WASHINGTON 8. D. C.
September 24, 1946
The Honorable William L. Clayton
Acting Secretary of State
Washington, D.C.
My dear Mr. Secretary:
As you are aware, the Far Eastern Commission has been consider
/9-2446
ing the Japanese Draft Constitution officially during the past sever-
al weeks. During this period the various representatives on the Com-
mission have been stating the positions of their respective govern-
ments. They have frequently pointed out that important matters which
normally would be included in a Constitution, such as the composition
of the House of Peers, are to be determined by subsequent legislation.
It has been the general opinion that such implementing laws should be
119FEAC
made available to the Commission for review since they would be an es-
C
sential part of the new constitutional structure which is being erected
S
AI
in Japan. The Commission believes that it is competent to pass upon
both the new Constitution and the implementing laws to ascertain whether
they are inconsistent in any respect with the Potsdam Declaration and
E3
the policy decisions of the Far Eastern Commission.
I have agreed with the other members of the Commission in thr
point of view, and accordingly stated at the 24th Meeting of the Com-
mission on August 28, 1946, and again at the 26th Meeting on September
19. 1946, that subsequent implementing legislation would be filed with
DCR NE Unit
the
11.
ga
1151

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)
137
-2-
the Commission by the United States Government and that the Commission
might pass a policy decision in regard to any aspect of implementing
legislation which the Commission agreed was inconsistent with the prin-
ciples of the Potsdam Declaration or relevant Commission policy deci-
sions.
Your attention is invited to this commitment on behalf of the United
States Government. It would be my understanding that the following pro-
cedure is in order. Draft implementing legislation on important subjects
would be filed with the Commission as it is submitted to the Diet. The
Commission may review such legislation if it involves policies within
the jurisdiction of the Commission, and may pass a policy decision in
regard to it if the commission should deem such decision necessary to
make the legislation consistent with the Potsdam Declaration and the pol-
icy decisions of the Far Eastern Commission. Such a policy decision would
be sent to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers through the usual
means of a directive issued by the United States Government.
A list is enclosed of the titles of bills reported in the Japanese
press as constituting the principal items of implementing legislation
for the Constitution. I understand that these bills are to be intro-
duced at an extraordinary session of the Diet this fall. These might
be requested from the Supreme Commander for file with the Commission as
the first step in honoring our conmitment.
Sincerely yours,
Frank R. McCoy
Chairman

DRAFT LETTER
My dear General McCoy:
In reference to your letter of September 24, 1946 to
the Acting Secretary of State concerning the discussions in
the Commission on the Japanese draft Constitution and related
subjects, I note with interest the faot that you have stated
to the Commission on two separate occasions that subsequent
1mplementing legislation of the Constitution would be filed
with the Commission by the United States Government and that
the Commission might pass a policy decision in regard to any
aspects of implementing le 1slation which the Commission
agreed was incons1stent with the Potsdam Declaration or
relevant Commission policy decisions.
I also note that you believe the appropriate procedure
18 to file with the Commission dreft implementing legislation
on importent subjects as it is submitted to the Diet, that
the Commission might review such legislation if it involves
policies within the jurisdiction of the Commission and might
pass policy decisions in regard to 1t 1f the Commission deems
such action necessary.
While I asree with the statements you have made before
te Commicnton tat tn lem ntinr leslslation would be filed
with the

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.95)
141
8
-2
with the Commission and that the Commission has the right to
pass a policy decislon within its competence in regard to
such legislation, I question the advisability of enoouraging
action by the Commission on drafts of such legislation.
While it would be advantageous for the Commission to reoeive
draft legislation for its information, 1t would be unfortunate,
if the Commission were to assume that it had the right to
make policy decisions on implementing legislation prior to
its enactment by the Diet. Any such right of rev1ew of draft
1 lslation, as opposed to legislation which has been enacted,
Would inevitably place the Commission in the position of
interferring with the normal legislative processes in Japan.
It would be consistent with the statements you have made
before the Commiss1on and with the authority of the Commis-
sion under its Terms of Heference, for the Commission to
review such legislation after it had been enacted providing,
of course, that cuch a policy decision was within the Commis-
sion's competence. Naturally, such policy decisions would
be transmitted to SCAP through the estaolished channels.
I have noted your enclosed titles of bille consituting
the principle items or implementin legialatton for the
Jonstltution and sur est that  request from coat for such
Leiulatron me ntt  made thr a con sultetive
nPBe

"formulate the policies, principles and standards in conformity with
which the fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of
Surrender may be accomplished."
894.011/10-146
It is further provided that when such decisions are made
by the Far Eastern Comnission, "The United States Government shall
prepare directives in accordance with the policy decisions of the
Commission and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through
the appropriate United States Government agency."
Confidential Fi
At the twenty-eighth meeting of the Far Eastern Commission
held at 2516 lassachusette Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D. C., on

Policies Relating to e New Japanese Constitution was unanimously
approved.

and transmitted to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms(D
of Reference.

from the minutes of the twenty-eighth meeting of the Far Eastern Com-

Japanese Constitution, that all cabinet ministers should be

House of Councillore should not have any predominance over the

EXCERPT FROM THE MINUTES OF THE TWENTY-KIGHTH MEETING
OF THE FAR EASTERN COMMISSION ON 25 SEPTEMBER 1946
ITEM 5 - STATEENT BY CO MITTEE NO. 3: CONSTITUTIONAL AND LEGAL REFORM,
ON FEC-O87/5, SOVIET PROPOSAIS CONCERNING THE DRAFT CONSTITU-
TION (FEC-087/6)
SIR CARL BERENDSEN moved pro forma and MR. VESUGAR seconded the
894.011/10-146
motion that the report of Committee No. 3 on the amendments to the draft
constitution proposed by the Soviet representative in FEC-087/5 be
adopted. In the light of the discuscion and action recorded below, SIR
CARL BERENDSEN, with the consent of Mr. Vesugar, later withdrew his pro
forma motion.
THE COnISSICN considered seriatim the statement of Soviet views
(FEC-087/5) in regard to the draft constitution, together with the re-
port by Committee No. 3 on these views (FEC-087/6).
ADMIRAL RAMISHVILI moved the adoption of paragraph 1 of FEC-O87/5
as follows:
"The Sovereign power of the people should be defined in the consti-
tution more precisely and clearly. The provision to the effect that
'sovereign power resides with the people' should constitute a separate
Article and have no connection with the definition of the role of the
Emperor."
The motion lapsed for lack of a second.
AD IRAL RAiISHVILI moved the adoption of the first sentence of
paragraph 2 of FEC-087/5 as follows:
"The Diet should have the right to afpoint not only the prime min-
ister, but also the remaining ministers of the Cabinet."
The motion lapsed for lack of a second.
DR. KOO moved and MAJOR FLIMSOIL seconded the motion that the prin-
ciple in paragraph 2 of FEC-087/5 that "All the ministers should be
civilians

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
145
civilians" be adopted, in the light of the favorable report on this
point by Committee No. 3.
SIR CARL BERENDSEN said that unanimous agreement on thie principle
already existed. He pointed out that the Commission had already laid
dowm this principle in its statement of basic principles for a new Jape-
nese constitution (FEC-O3l/19) and that the Supreme Commander, according
to the communication from him (FEC-087/8) considered earlior on the
agenda, had obtained the agreement of the Japanese Government to this
principle. SIR CARL said that he realized that the Soviet representa-
tive entertained doubt as to whether the Diet would include the desired
provision in the constitution. He thought it not likely that the Diet
would challenge the authority of the Supreme Commander in this manner,
and he suggested that steps to meet such a contingency need not be taken
now.
GENERAL MCCOY said that, although the principle had been included
in the statement of basic constitutional principles, there was no harm
in reaffirming it.
The motion by Dr. Koo was carried unanimously.
AD IRAL RAMISHVILI moved the adoption of paragraph 3 of FEC-O87/5
as follows:
"iembers of the Supreme Court should be elected by the Diet for 5
years service."
The motion lapsed for lack of a second.
ADMIRAL RAMISHVILI moved the adoption of paragraph 4 of FEC-O87/5
as follows:
"The Article concerning regency should be worded in the sense that
regency is established on the principle of the free expression of will
of
Encl. "B"
-2-

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
146
of the people with whom the sovereign power resides. Such prineiple of
establishing the regency is in compliance with the spirit of the Pote-
dam Declaration and also with the decision of the Far Eastern Comission
on 'Basic Principles of a New Japanese Constitution,' in whioh it is
stated that 'the Japanese people should be encouraged to abolish the
Emperor Institution or to reform it along more democratic lines.w
The motion lapsed for lack of a second.
ADMIRAL RAMISHVILI moved and DR. DE KAT seconded the motion that
paragraph 5 of FEC-087/6 be adopted as follows:
"The Committee agrees that the House of Councillors should not have
any predominance over the House of Representatives and considers that it
will be essential to scrutinize the implementing legislation very care-
fully to ensure that such predominance shall not be established.
1he motion was carried unanimously.
SIR CARI. BERENDSEN moved and MR. VESUGAR seconded the motion that
the Commission reaffirm its previous decision taken in FEC-031/19,
Basic Principles for a New Japanese Constitution, that all cabinet
ministers should be civilians and decide as a matter of policy that the
House of Councillors should not have any predominance over the House of
Representatives and that the Commission state that it considers essen-
tial its continuing right to scrutinize the implementing legislation
very carefully to ensure that such predominance not be established.
The motion was carried unanimously.
THE CO:MISSION unanimously agreed to table FEC-087/6.
ADMIRAL RAMISHVILI presented the following formal statement:
"Proceeding from the desire to bring the draft of the Japanese Con-
stitution into full compliance with the Potsdam Declaration and the de-
cision of the Far Eastern Commission: FEC-O31/19, the Soviet Delogation
has made the following proposals at the 26th meeting of the Far Eastern
Commission:
(a) The provision to the effect that 'sovereign power resides in
the people' should constitute a separate Article, and not in connection
with the definition of the role of the Emperor.
(b) To
Encl. B"
-3-

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
147
(b) To recognise the right of the Dist to appoint not only the
prime minister but also the remaining Cabinet ministers.
(c) All the ministers, including the prime minister, should be
civilians.
(d) The members of the Supreme Court ahould be elected by the Diet
for 5 years service.
(e) The Constitution Article concerning regency should be worded
in the sense that regency is established on the principle of the free
expression of will of the Jaranese people with whom the sovereign power
resides.
(f) The USSR Delegation doesn't insist on the unicameral system
provided the House of Councillors has no predominance over the House of
Representatives.
"The Soviet Delegation is convinced that the above proposals are
in full accord with the principles set out in the Potsdam Declaration
and with the decisions of the Far Eastern Commission: FEC-O3l/19, and
represent the recessary provisions of a truly democratic Constiution.
"However, the proposals of the Soviet Delegation under (a), (b),
(d) and (e) have been rejected by the majority of the members of the
Far Eastern Commission.
"Under these circumstances, the Soviet Delegation, acting on be-
half of the Government of the USSR, states that it does not consider it
possible to approve the draft of the Japanese Constitution, as the lat-
ter, without inclusion of the above-mentioned proposals, does not comply
with the principles set out in the Potsdam Declaration nor with the de-
cision of the Far Eastern Commission: FEC-031/19.
"The Soviet Delegation reserves its right of criticism and consid-
eration in future of the questions concerning the Japanese Constitution."

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
SUPPLEMENTARY CABLE
The directive forwarded you in War-Y, to which this message is a
supplement for your information, was passed under circumstances some-
what different from normal Commiasion procedure.
You will note that in the directive the Commission reaffirms its
previous decision that all cabinet ministers should be civilians. This
reaffirmation was agreed after receipt of your message (War-Z) to the
effect that you had persuaded the Japanese Government to write such a
provision into the Constitution. There was some question within the
U. S. Government as to whether there was any need to send such a re-
affirmation to you as a directive in view of your statement that you
had taken the necessary steps. In view of the fact, however, that you
have taken this action and consequently no harm can be done by repeat-
ing this policy to you, and of the further fact that the Commission
expects their reaffirmation to be conveyed you, the reaffirmation is
included in the directive in order that the United States may fulfill
its obligations with the Commission according to paragraph III, 1 of
the Terms of Reference of the Commission.
As to the Commission's other decision, that the House of Councillors
should not have any predominance over the House of Representatives, there
was similarly some question in the U. S. Government as to whether the
Commission was merely making a statement of its owm intentions or whether
it intended this policy to be conveyed you as a directive. Since this
matter will be of some concern to you in any case, and since you should be
aware of the Commission's concern in this connection, again, this policy
decision is being transmitted you as a directive in accordance with para-
graph III,l of the Terms of Reference.
The last sentence of the directive, with respect to implementing
legislation, is another matter of which you should be aware in order
that there may be appropriate coordination between the Commiasion and
yourself.
In accordance with paragraph III, 4 of the Terms of Reference, the
U. S. Government intends to file with the Commission only the directive
which is being sent you but not repeat not this supplementary cable.

The Terms of Reference of the Far Eastern Commission provide
that one of the funotions of the Commission should be to "formulate
894.011/10-1746
the policies, principles and standards in conformity with whioh the
fulfillment by Japan of its obligations under the Terms of Surrender
may be accomplished."
It is further provided that when such decisions are made by
the Far Eastern Commission, "The United States Government shall prepare
direotives in accordance with the policy decisions of the Commission
and shall transmit them to the Supreme Commander through the appropriate
United States Government ageney."
At the thirtieth meeting of the Far Eastern Commission held
at 2516 bassachusetts Avenue, Northwest, Washington, D. C., on 17
October 1946, the enclosed polioy decision relative to Provisions for
the Review of a New Japanese Constitution was unanimously approved.
As Secretary General of the Far Eastern Commission, I have
been instructed to forward this decision to you on behalf of the Com-
mission, in order that the appropriate directives may be prepared and
transmitted to the Supreme Commander in accordance with the Terms of
Reference.
Before the enclosed policy decision was formally approved by
the Commission, the United States member made a formal statement, recall-
ing the position of his government to the effect that the United States
was prepared to agree to the enclosed policy "provided that the Com-
mission at some subsequent meeting and after obtaining the view of the
Supreme Commander will consider the time and manner of issuance of the
policy decision embodied in the paper." The United States member's
statement explained that the enclosed polioy would be forwarded to the
United States Government to be transmitted as a directive to the Supreme
