=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
ENCLOSURE
PROVISIONS FOR THE REVIEW OF A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
1. The new constitution, which will in due season after
promulgation become the legal successor of the present constitution
with such changes as have been made or may be made as a result of
consideration and polioy decision of the Far Bastern Commission,
shall be subjeot to further review by the Diet and the Far Eastern
Commission in terms of the following paragraph.
2. In order that the Japanese people may have an opportunity,
after the new constitution goes into effeot, to reoonsider it in the
light of the experience of its working, and in order that the Far
Eastern Commission may satisfy itself that the constitution fulfills
the terms of the Potsdam Declaration and other controlling doouments,
the Commission decides as a matter of policy that, not sooner than
one year and not later than two years after it goes into effeot, the
situation with respect to the new constitution should be reviewed by
the Diet. Without prefudice to the continuing jurisdiotion of the
Far Eastern Commission at any time, the Commission shall also review
the constitution within this same period. The Far Eastern Commission,
in determining whether the Japanese constitution is an expression of
the free will of the Japanese people, may require a referendum or
some other appropriate procedure for ascertaining Japanese opinion
with respect to the oonstitution.
FEC-031/40

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.98)
158
EXCERPT FROM THE MINUTES OF THE THIRTIETH MEETING OF THE FEC. 17 OCT. I
CONFIDENTIAL
ITEM 4 - PROVISIONS FOR THE REVIEN OF A NEN JAPANESE CONSTITUTION (PEC-O5IA)
GENERAL MoCOY said that informal consultation participated in by
the Australian, Soviet, and United States representatives had resulted in
the agreement by those three on the present version of the review paper.
MR. MAkIN said that the only remaining difference of opinion was
as to the time of announcement to the Japanese of the adoption of the review
prinoiple. He recalled that ever since the original proposal for review was
made by Dr. Evatt the general opinion on the Commission had been that annoumoe-
ment should follow immediately upon adoption of the policy by the Comission.
The United States Government, however, had sinoe doubted the wisdom of this
course and had advocated postponement of announcement. The Australian view,
he continued, was that announoement of the policy should take place im-
mediately following its adoption. To delay announcement, he pointed out,
might lay the Commission open to the charge from the Japanese that it had
broken faith by not declaring to them the existence of the review provision,
211.0110-1746
particularly since the Japanese were planning elaborate ceremonies to
celebrate the new constitution.
Despite this conviotion on the part of his government, MR. MAKIN
said, he was prepared, in order to secure unanimous adoption of the paper,
to consent to delay in the time of the announcement to the Japanese of
adoption of the policy. He agreed that the Supreme Commander should be
consulted on the question of publication. He urged, however, that suoh
announcement take place as soon as possible, and that it not be delayed
later than the date of promulgation of the constitution.
SIR CARL BERENDSEN expressed general agreement with the viows
of lir. Lakin. He also expressed general agreement with the present dooument
and was, indeed, pleased to see that the Commission had reached an agreement
on the affirmation of the principle of review. He asked what diffioulties
the United States Government now saw in connection with the immediate announoe-
ment of the adoption of this paper. He conceded that it would have been unwise
to announce such a policy while the constitution was under consideration by
the Japanese Diet and he granted, furthermore, that it might be unwise to
announce the decision before promulgation of the constitution. However, he
said, he could not understand why the United States Government deemed it unwiso
to announce the review principle at the time of promulgation, and he asked
for an explanation of the United States position on this point. GENERAL MoCOY
replied that numerous reasons had existed for the United States position.
One of these reasons had been consideration for the diffioult position of
the Supreme Commander who had felt that nothing should be done whioh, in the
oyes of the Japanese, would condemn a constitution at the moment of ite
adoption by them. Other reasons for the United States position had been of
a legal nature, and these points had now been met by the present version of
paragraph 1.

GENERAL MoCOY presented the following formal statement:
"At the meeting of the Commission on September 2lst, I stated
that my Government is prepared to accept the Review paper provided
that the Commission at some subsequent meeting and after obtaining
the view of the Supreme Commander will consider the time and manmer
of issuance of the policy decision embodied in the paper. That iss
if the pending paper is approved by the Commission, as we trust it
will be, the next step, after transmitting this paper as a polioy
decision to the United States Government for communication as a
directive to the Supreme Commander, will be to request the views
of the Supreme Commander as to the time and manner of issuanoe of
this policy decision. After the views of the Supreme Commander have
been received, the Commission will then be in a position to consider
the time and manner of the issuance of this policy decision.
"After this Review paper is passed, I should be pleased to be
authorized to consult with the Supreme Commander in order to obtain
his views on this subject."
DR. PATTERSON asked whether the reservation respecting "issuance"
meant that the forwarding of a directive to the Supreme Commander would be
delayed. GENERAL MoCOY replied that the directive to the Supreme Commander
would go forward immediately, but that the time of issuanoe in Japan and
publicity was a question for continued consideration when the views of the
Supreme Commander had been received.
GENERAL LoCOY moved and MR. MAKIN seconded the motion that the
Commission adopt FEC-031/40, with the understanding that the Chairman be
authorized to consult with the Supreme Commander as to the Supreme Com-
mander's views regarding the time and manner of issuance of the policy
decision and that after the Supreme Commander's views had been received
the Commission would reconsider the time and manner of such issuance. The
motion was carried unanimously.
GENERAL MoCOY said that the foreoing views of kr. Makin and
Sir Carl Berendsen would be communicated to the Supreme Commander at the
time of requestin his views as to the time and manner of issuance of the
policy decision.

DIRECTIVE TO THE SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
DIRECTIVE REGARDING PROVISIONS FOR THE REVIEW
OF A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
The following directive, prepared by the State Department
to implement the policy adopted by the Far Eastern Commission
on 17 October 1946 under the prov1sions of paragraph II, A, 1,
of its terms of reference, has been received from the State,
War, and Navy Depertments for transmission to you for your
guidance in accordance with paragraph III, 1, of those terms of
reference:
"1. The new constitution, which will in due season
after promulgation become the legal successor of the present
constitutlon with such changes as have been made or may be
made as a result of consideration and policy decision of the
Far Eastern Commission, shall be subject to further review by
the Diet and the Far Eastern Commission in terms of the
following paragraph.
"2. In order that the Japenese people may have an oppor-
tunity, after the new constitution goes into effect, to re-
consider it in the light of the experience of its working,
and in order that the Far Eastern Commission may satisfy
itself that the constitution fulfills the terms of the Potsdam
Declaration and other controlling documents, the Commission
decides as a matter of policy that, not sooner than 1 year

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.98)
RESTRICTED
and not later than 2 years after it goes into effect, the
situatlon with respect to the new constitution should be re-
viewed by the Diet. Without prejudice to the continuing
jurisdiction of the Far Eastern Commission at any time, the
Commission shell also review the constitution within this
same period. The Far Eastern Commission, in determining
whether the Japanese constitution is an expression of the
frec will of the Japanese people, may require a referendum
or some other &ppropriate procedure for escertaining Japanese
opinion with respect to the constitution."

Enclosure No. 1 to despatch No. 685 dated Nevember . 1946 frem the

COPY
CHINESE MISSION IN JAPAN
TOKYO
October 25, 1946
His Excellency
George Atcheson, Jr.,
Chairman, Allied Council for Japan,
Tokyo, Japan.
Dear Ambassador,
According to reports appearing in the Japanese newspapers, the
new constitution of Japan will be promulgated on November 3, which
is the day set by the Japanese for their annual celebration of the
Meiji-setsu or Meiji festival. Marked by two aggressive wars against
Japan's continental neighbors, the Meiji period is remembered by the
Japanese people chiefly for the success achieved in the expansion of
their empire. It is suggested for this reason that November 3 is
hardly an auspicious day for the promulgation of the new constitution
which is intended to serve as the political foundation of a democratic
Japan. If you concur with me in this interpretation, I hope that
means will be found to persuade the Japanese Government to select
a more auspicious day for the event.
Yours very sincerely,
/s/ Chu Shih-ming

COPY
ALLIED COUNCIL FOR JAPAN
TOKYO
Office of the Hember
for
The United States of America
October 31, 1946.
Dear General:
With reference to your letter of October 25, 1946, concern-
ing the selection of November 3 as the date for promulgation of
the new Japanese Constitution, it is my understanding that this
day was chosen by the Japanese Government because the Emperor
Meiji was mainly responsible for the first Japanese Constitution.
It does not seem to me that the choice of this date has any
far-reaching significance and, in my opinion, it would accordingly
not seem desirable that steps be taken to intervene in what is re-
garded as a matter for local administration.
Yours very sincerely.
/s/ George Atcheson, Jr.
His Exceilency
Liouionant General Chu Shih-ming,
etc.. etc., etc.,
Chief, Chinese Mission in Japan,
Tokyo.

"Before the policy decision regarding provisions for
the review of a new Japanese constitution (Serial No. 62)
was formally approved by the Far Eastern Commission on
October 17, 1946, Ambassador Makin, Australian representa-
tive on the Far Eastern Commission, stated with reference
to the announcement of the policy decision that the Austral-
ian view was that the announcement should take place immedi-
ately following Its adoption. However, Mr. Makin agreed
that the Supreme Commander should be consulted on the yues-
tion of publication. Sir Carl Berendsen, the New Zealand
representative, expressed general agreement with the views
of Mr. Makin.
"The U.S. Member made the formal statement to the
effect that the U.S. was prepared to agree to the policy
decision 'provided that the Commission at some subsequent
meeting and after obtaining the view of the Supreme Commander
will consider a time and manner of Issuance of the policy
decision embodied in the paper.'

SUBJECT:
Time and Manner of Issuance of Far Eastern Commission
Policy Statement on Review of Constitution.
4.011/11-546
In my report on pending problems on Japan of November 4,
I noted that the Chairman of the FEC had requested, at the
FEC meeting of November 1, that Committee #3 prepare a state-
ment for the Chairman to use as basis for a consultative
message between the Chairman and SCAP on the question of
issuance. Committee #3 met on November 5 at whioh time the
following arose:
(1) After general discussion of SCAP's reply to FEC
in which he strongly recommended against publication of the
policy decision at least for a year, the Committee decided
to transmit for SCAP's information verbatim extracts of the
minutes of FEC meetings of October 3l and November 1, with
a view to informing him of the reasons why several members
of the Commission favor 1ssuance by SCAP of the FEC decision
in the immediate future.
(2) The United Kingdom member proposed that a policy
decision of the Comnission be transmitted to SCAP as follows:
"The Commission considers that it is now neceesary to acquaint
the Japanese pl with the terms of the policy decision of
October 17th and request the United States Government to
direct SCAP to communicste the terms of the Commission policy
formally to the Japanese Government before the date of the
meeting of the special session of the Diet on November 25."
(3) There was considerable difference of opinion as to
whether discretion should be left to SCAP and the Japanese
Government to determine whether the Japanese people should
be told that any such decision was that of the Far Eastern
Commission. No decisive vote was taken on this point.
(4) Representatives of China, the Netherlands, the
Philippines, and the United States concurred in the proposal
that further consultation be undertaken with SCAP on the time
and manner of 1ssuance of the pclicy decision and that his
DC/R
viws be obtained on the various alternative methods as to
894.
how the statement might be 1ssued.
Anal
(5) This proposal was opposed by other members of the
Committee who argued that such consultation had already taken
Rev
place and had produced no favorable result, that SCAP's message
disregarded

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)
171
-2-
disregarded the decision of the Commission and implied that
he was opposed to the policy deoision, and that if any aotion
was to be taken on November 25 there was not suffio1ent time
for consultation.
(6) The Committee voted 7 to 4 in favor of a polioy
decision being issued to SCAP by the U.S. Government order-
ing him to inform the Japanese Government of the contents of
the FEC decision on review of the Constitution.
(7) The divergent views of the Committee will be
presented to the Commission when it next meets, probably
on November 14.
It was obvious from the tone of the discussion that
many members of the Commission, notably U.K., Australia,
France, New Zealend and U.S.S.R. feel strongly that a further
directive should be sent to General MacArthur covering the
question of time and manner of issuance of the policy on
review of the Constitution. The question arises as to
whether:
(1) U.S. member of the FEC should be instruoted by
SWNCC to veto any attempt to issue a policy decision
on the question of time and manner of issuance of
the review paper;
(2) SWNCC should prepare a draft of a consultative
message to SCAP on this subject which would be aoceptable
to the United States and which could be submitted to
the Commission at its next meeting;
(3) Any other alternative action should be taken.

As you will note by the attached FEC document,
the Australian Representative at the meeting of the
Commission on November 2l formally raised the question
of the procedure by which the Commission would pass
upon legislation to implement the Japanese Constitution.
Major Plimeoll stated that he was not raising the
question in the spirit of criticism but only to place
it upon the record and to be sure that the Commission
would have ample time to examine draft leg1slation
qu
hefore 1ts enactment by the Japanese Diet. General
MacCoy stated that every effort was being made to
obtain copies of Japanese araft legislation and that
the War Department had sent an official to Tokyo to
look into the matter of obtaining all necessary drafts.
It 1s apparent that most members of the Commission
take for ranted that they will be permitted to examine
and pass upon imolementing legislation. Nothing has
been said in any Commission meeting to indicate that
8
the United States Government might oppose such a course.
In fact, Genersl MacCoy has on at least two occasions
ind cated thet the Commission would have the opportunity
h
tg examine draft leislation and to pass a policy de-
diricn if such legislation were found to be inconsistent
0
th Potsdam or controllin documents.

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.97)
Competence of Far Eastern Commission with regard to Legis-
lation ImpIementing the Japanese Constitution
THE PROBLEM
1. To determine competence of Far Eastern Commission
to pass upon legislation passed in implementation of the
Japanese Constitution, and methods of dealing with such
legislation.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See Appendix "A".
1
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
d
894.011
CONCLUSION
4. It is concluded that:
a.
The Far Eastern Commission may pass policy
dec sions which formulate policies, principles, and
standards to ich legislation implementing the
Japanese Constitution must conform.
b! The Far Eastern Commission may review draft
legislation at any time subsequent to its introduction
into the Japanese Diet solely for the purpose of deter-
mining whether such legislation is inconsistent with
the Fotsdam Declaration, controlling documents or
decisions of the Far Eastern Commission.
c. In order that there may be continuing consul-
tation between SCAP and the FTC on the subject of
implementing legislation and that both FEC and SCAP
may be completely informed of developments in the
Japanese Diet and FEC respectively, formal liaison
be established between FEC and SCAP to consist
of an officer selected by SCAP to be sent to

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2-
Washington and a representative of FEc (presumably a
member of the Seoretariat) to be attached to SCAP in
Tokyo.
d. If the Far Eastern Commission determines that
a draft law 1s inconsistent with the Potsdam deolara-
tion, other controlling documents or a decision of the
Far Eastern Commission, it may pass a policy decision
to this effect.
RECOMMENDATIONS
5.
the conclusions in paragraph 4 above be tran to the
United States member, Far Eastern Commission, for his
guidance.

APPENDIX "A"
Facts Bearing on the Problem
1. Excerpts of Report of Committee No. 3 on its Study
of the Draft Constitution (FEC O87/2):
"b. Implementing Legislation.
The Committee notes
that the draft Constitution leaves many matters to be
determined by subsequent legislation. This is of
particular importance with regard to the status of
the House of Councillors. The Committee considers,
therefore, that if such matters are to be left for
determination in subsequent legislation, drafts of
such lepislation should be made available for the
Far Eastern Commission as soon es these drafts are
submitted to the Diet."
2. Excerpts from Ninutes of the 24th Neeting of the
Far Eactern Cormission, Aupust 28, 1946.
"With respect to paragraph b, Implementing Legis-
lation, of FEC-O&7/2, GENERAI NCCOY said that it was
now the reneral understanding that drafts of legisla-
tion before the Diet would be made available to the
Commission."
3. Excerpts from Minutes of the 26th Meeting of the
Far Eastern Commission, September 19, 1946.
"GENERAL ICCOY stated that his Government favored
a constitution comprised of general principles which
would depend for their execution on subsequent imple-
menting legislation. Such implementing legislation,
he said, would be filed with the Commission and the
Commission might approve a subsequent policy dectsion
on any aspect of implementing legislation which seemed

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
to violate the principles of the Potsdam Declaration."
4. In a policy decision approved by the Far Eastern
Commission on September 25, 1946, the Commission referred
to its right to scrutinize implementing legislation to
ensure that predominence of the House of Councillors over
the House of Representatives is not established.
"The Far Eastern Commission reaffirms its previous
decision, taken in FEC-031/19, /Directive Serial No. 547
Basic Principles for a New Japanese Constitution, that
all cabinet ministers should be civilians, end further
decides as a matter of policy that the House of Council-
lors should not have any predominance over the House
of Representatives. The Commission considers essential
its continuing right to scrutinize the implementing
legislation very carefully to insure that such
predominance is not established."
5. On April 12, 1946 the chairman of FEC requested
the U.S. Government to consult with SCAP on the subject
of sending to washington a staff officer to confer with
the Commission on the subject of constitutional reform.
An excerpt from the State Department's reply (FEC O318)
follows:
"a reply has now been received from General
MacArthur in which he states that he is in full agree-
ment with the need for e closer working arrangement
and understanding between SCAP and the Commission
and stands ready to do everything in his power to
further this end. He states, however, that it is
impossible for him to send an officer to act as his
deputy in the broad matters involving constitutional
reform, as he has given his personal attention to
this question and there is no officer in a position
to express in detail his views."

dec1sion of

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.99)
APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. The Far Eastern Commission, by its terms of refer-
ence, may formulate policies, principles, and standards in
conformity with which the fulfillment by Japan of its
obligations under the terms of surrender may be accomplished.
Among these obligations of Japan 1s the passage by the
Japanese Diet of legislation implementing the Constitution
which itself has been enacted in conformity with Far
Eastern Comrission policy decisions. It therefore follows
that the Far Eastern Commission may pass policy decisions
with regard to implementing legislation as it has done in
the case of the Constitution.
2. hen legislation implementing the Constitution
is introduced into the Japanese Diet, it is assumed that
copies of draft bills vill be sent as a matter of informa-
tion bv SCAP to the Commission. Any member of the Commis-
sion should have the right to bring before the Commission,
or an appropriate committee thereof, provisions in such
draft legislation which such member may deem to be incon-
sistent with the Potsdem Decleration, the controlling docu-
ments, or decisions of the Far Eastern Commission. The
Commission thereupon may examine such draft legislation
solely for the purpose of deciding this question of con-
sistency. It may not redraft Japanese legislation, suggdst
additions to laws, or take any action beyond that described
above.
3. If the right of the Far Eastern Commission to
review implementing legislation is conceded, as stated in
2 above, the question to be determined is whether this
review should take place before or after the legislation

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
is enacted by the Japanese Diet. If the Far Eastern Com-
mission, by formal voting procedure, decides thet a provi-
sion of a draft law introduced into the Diet is inconsistent
with Potsdam, controlling documents, or policy decisions
of the Far Bastern Commission, it may pass a policy decision
to that effect as the basis for a directive to be sent to
SCAP. If such a directive is sent to SCAP while the legis-
lation in question is still under consideration by the Diet,
it will be possible to obtain necessary changes bv SCAP
action, before the legislation is finally enacted by the
Diet. In such instances, the action of the Far Eastern
Commissicn will not come to the public notice of the
Japanese and no action will be necessary which would in
fact declare invalid laws enacted by the duly constituted
representatives of the Japanese people in accordance with
the Constitution. In the case of the Constitution, the
Far Eastern Commission was able to scrutinize the draft
and to make certain suggestions which resulted in changes
in the draft obtained without publicity or derogation to
the legal legislative rrocesses in Janan.
4. If the Far Eastern Commission should decide,
after a law is duly enacted by the Japanese Diet thet such
law is inconsistent with controlling documents or Far East-
ern Commission policy decisions, it thereby assumes the
right to veto and declare invalid acts of a legislative
body set up under a Constitution passed in conformity to
decisions of the Far Eastern Commission. Such action on
the part of the Fer Bastern Commission would render the
Diet powerless in the eyes of the Japanese people, would
destroy public faith in the organs of government estab-
lished under the new constitution, would discourage the

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
growth of democratic processes and would strengthen elemonts
which might at some future time endeavor to repeal the con-
stitution and laws established under it. If the Far Bastorn
Commission had taken e ction following final passage of the
Constitution in the House of Peers, the result would have
been exactly that which SCAp hes been so anxious to avoid,
the open dictation to the Japanese Government by the vic-
torious powers tc invalidate a constitution duly enacted
bv representatives of the Japanese neople. In the case
of implementing legislation, the exercise of the Far
Eastern Commission's right to d etermine the consistency
cf such legisletion after its enactment, would obtain
the same result unless the United States were to use its
veto to prevent the action. If, however, the United States
accepts the principle that the Far Tastern Commission has
this right of review, it can use its veto power only on
the  erits of the case, and not to negate a principle
which it has accepted.
5. Although SCAt refused to send a liaison officer
to confer vith the FEC on the subject of the Jap Constitu-
tion, he did so on the grounds that there wes no officer
in SCAp Headouarters in a position to express General Mac-
Arthur's views in detail. It is not believed that SCAP
would necessarily object to continuing liaison, the purpose
of which wculd not he to choose individuals who would speak
for SCAP in Washington or for FEC in Tokyo but to assure
efficient exchange of information and full end free exchange
of views between FEC and SCAP. General NacArthur's expres-
sion of Fagreement with the need for a closer working ar-
rangement and understanding between SCAP and the Commission"

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
and his statement that he "stands ready to do everything
in his power to further this end" suggest that he might
be agreeable to liaison of the type proposed in this paper.
6. It is envisaged that formal liaison would result
in more efficient servicing of FEC w'th draft documents
and information on legislative developments in Japan.
Provided with full information, end e better opportunity
for informal consultation, there would be less likelihood
that the Commission members would come to the point of
pressing for rolicy decisions on points in implementing
legislation. Discussions in FEC meetings have revealed
that the principel concern of most members is that of
being informed well in advance of legislation under
consideration in the Diet. They do not wish to be
surprised by & fait eccompli as they were in the case
of the Constitution.
7. In view of the fact that the Australian Repre-
sentative of the Far Eastern Commission has officially
reised the question of FEC procedure toward implementing
legislation, it 1s necessery that the United States Gov-
ernment formulate a decislon in regard to this matter.

of the new Japanese constitution, issued a special statement

it has its frailties but by and lar e it shows how far we have

forward toward world peace and oodwill and normalcy."

publication of the policy decision contained in Enclosure "A"
of FEC O31/41 (Appendix "A").
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM AND DISCUSSION
2. See Appendics "A" and "B".
CONCLUSION
3. It is concluded that the U. S. position should be as
follows:
"The U. S. Government will not agree to the publioation
of the Policy Dccision contained in FEC O31/4l until,
after consultation with General MacArthur, the U. S.
Government concludes that such publication is consistent
with U. S. foreign policy and the accomplishment of
Allied objectives in Japan."
RECOMMENDATIONS

a. SWNCC approve the statoment of the U. S. position
as set forth in the Conclusion; and
b. The Conclusion be forwarded by the Department of
State to the U. S. Member on the Far Eastern Commission
as a statement of the U. S. position.

ENCLOSURE"A"
PROVISIONS FOR THE REVIEW OF A NEW JAPANESE CONSTITUTION
1. The new constitution, whioh will in due season after
promulgation become the legal successor of the present constitu-
tion with such changes as have been made or may be made as a
result of consideration and policy decislon of the Far Eastern
Commission, shall be suuject to further revlew by the Diet and
the Far Easterr Commission in terms of the following paragraph.
2. In order that the Japanese people may have an opportunity,
after the new constitution goes into effeot, to reoonsider it
ir. the light of the experience of its working, and in order that
the Far E stern Commisslon may satiafy itself that the constitu-
tion fulfills the terms of the Potsdam Declaration and other
controlling doouments, the Commiesion decides as a matter of
policy that, not sooner than one year and not later than two
years after it goes into effect, the situation with respect to
the new constitution sh-uld be reviewed by the Diet. Without
prejudice to the cortiruing jurisdiotion of the Far Eastern
Commission at any time, the Commisslon shall also review the
constitution within this same period. The Far Eastern Commission,
in determining whether the Japanese constitution is an expression
of the free will of the Japanece people, may require a referendum
or some other appropriate procedure for ascertaining Japanese
opinion with respect to the constitution.

"GENERAL MCCOY said that informal consultation participated

"MR. AKIN said that the only remaining difference of

He recalled that ever
since the original proposal for review was made by Dr. Evatt
the general opinion on the Commission had been that announcement
sh uld follow immediately upon adoption of the polioy by the
Commission. The United States Government, however, had since
doubted the wisdon of this course and had advocated postponement
of announcement. The Australian view, he continued, was that
annruncement of the policy should take place immediately follow
ing is adoption. To delay announcement, he pointed out, might
lay the Commission open to the charge from the Japanese that
it had broken faith by not declaring to them the existenoe of
the review provision, particularly since the Japanese were
planning elaborate ceremonies to celebrate the new constitution.
"Despite this conviction on the part of his Government,
MR. MAkIN said, he was prepared, in order to secure unanimous
adoption of the paper, to consent to delay in the time of the
announcement to the Japanese of adoption of the policy. He
agreed that the Supreme Commander should be consulted on the
question of publication. He urged, however, that such announoe
ment take place as soon as possible, and that it not be delayed
later than the date of promulgation of the constitution.

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
APPENDIX "B"
PROVISIONS FOR THE REVIEW OF A NEW JAPANESE
CONSTITUTION: REPLY OF THE SUPREME COMMANDER
FOR THE ALLIED POWERS TO CONSULTATION
I have received the suggestion or the Far Eastern Commission
that 1ts policy decisior regarding Provisions for the Review of
a New Japanese Constitution might be made publio. The purpose
underlying such a suggestion is obscure. As the polioy decision
in reference merely provides that some time after 18 months and
before 30 months "The situation with respect to the new consti-
tution should be reviewed by the Diet"; that the Far Eastern
Commission shall review the constitution within the same period;
and that the Far Eastern Commission may require a referendum or
some other appropriate procedure for ascertaining Japanese
opinion with respect to the constitution; it prescribes no aotion
whatsoever for at least 18 months or until the new constitution
has been in effect for one year. It is unrealistic in its
failure to recognize the inesoapable facts that by its very
terms the constitution is constantly before the Diet from its
effective date for any change or amendment that experience may
suggest or otherwise may be proposed, and that the power of
review or alteration of the instrument, or the enforcement of
any measure to subject it to additional tests of public opinion,
is inherent in the power of occupation itself, and will so
remain as long as the Allied Control of Japan is maintained.
Throughout the developme nt of the new conatitution, aotion
has been largely avoided whioh might be construed as compulsory
process in order that nothing might negate or compromise the
free character essential if the instrument 1s to live. The
publication of referenced statement of policy on the other hand
would inevitably result in just what we have thus scrupulously
tried to avoid. And for what purpose? It would instantly be
viewed in the publio mind as a display of force by the Allied
Powers. It would destroy the free oharaoter of the Charter and

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
its expression of the popular will by the coersive threat of
external force to be implied. It would reduce the very essonoo
of durability upon whioh the instrument has been built to a
frail skeleton of temporary expedience overshadowed by the
threat of forced abrogation or revision at the point of Allied
bayonets, with an open and continuing invitation to the foroes
of reaction to conserve and extend their political strength
against the time that, taking advantage of its very torms,
pr ssure might be brought upon the Diet or the people direot
to destroy the constitution and restore the old order which
Allied policy seeks permanently to auppress.
As the harmful effect of such publicity is as patently olear
as any benefit thercfrom to the Allied case is obscure, I most
stongly recommend against any such procedure at least until the
pronosed year of experience has passed and the Far Eastern
Conmission feels that the situation then existing calls for
affirmative action.
SWNCC 228/12
- 84-

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.98)
F4PEC101
TO
JA - Mr. Borton
DATE: December 31, 1946
PROM :
Le - C. E. Snow
SUBJECT:
Constitutionality of the Laws Implemarting the Japanese Constitution
1. You have asked Le for an opinion on the constitutionality"
of the Laws Implementing the Japanese Constitution,--in particular
of the Imperial House Law (FEC-lOl) and the Law for the Election
of the House of Councillors (FEC-lOl/1).
2. For the purpose of this opinion it is understood that you
intend to raise no question under the present Constitution of
Japan (February 1l, 1889) but that you refer only to the New Consti-
tution adopted by the Japanese Diet on November 3, 1946. So far as
the Constitution of 1889 is concerned, the New Constitution itself and
the Implementing Laws stand on the same footing. Both were enacted
by the National Diet, which declares itself to be the duly elected
representatives of the Japanese people. If the Diet has the power,
within the present Constitution, to enact a new Constitution, 
fortiori it has the same power to enact the implementing legislation
without which the New Constitution cannot be made to operate.
f
3. The New Constitution, by express provision, looks to the
passage of implementing legislation. Art. X provides that the quali-
94.0
fications for Japenese citizenship "shall be determined by law".
Art. II prcvides that the Imperial Throne shall be succeeded to
"in accordance with the Imperial House Law passed by the Diet".
Art. XLIII provides that the number of the members of each House
"shall be fixed by law". Art. XLIV provides thet the qualifications
of electors and members of toth Houses "shall be fixed by law"; and
Art. XIVII, that matters perteining to the election of members of both
Houses, electoral districts, and method of voting, "shall be fixed by
2
law". Until the requisite laws referred to by these Articles have
-3144
been passed, no new Diet, which is described as "the highest organ of
State power" and the "sole law-making authority of the State" can
be ever elected. Consequently, it is provided, in Art. C, that the
enactment of laws necessary for the enforcement of the Constitution;
the election of members of the House of Councillors and the procedure
for the convocation of the Diet "and other preper: tory procedures",
may be executed before the effective date of the enforcement of the
New Constitution. The implication is clear thet such laws can be
possed by the present Diet.
4. In

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
4. In this connection attention is invited to the pro-
vision in Art. CIII that members of the House of Representatives
and other public officials in office on the effective date of
the Constitution shall not forfeit their positions on such effeo-
tive date, nor until their successors are elected unless otherwise
specified by law". This provision clearly looks to a continuation
of the powers of the present government to the effective date of the
New Constitution with a carry-over to provide opportunity for elections
under the New Constitution.
5. Art. XCVIII of the New Constitution provides that the
Constitution "shall be the supreme law of the State", and that
no public law contrary to the provisions thereof "shall have legal
force or validity". Art. LXxxI designates the Supreme Court as the
court of last resort with power o determine the constitutionality
of any law, and Art XCIX enjoins on Emperor, Diet and Judges the
obligation to urholc the Constitution. Consequently, any implementing
legislation designed to carry over the effective date of the Constitu-
tion must be examined to make sure thet its provisions are not contrary
to the provisions of the Constituion. It is understood that you do
not desire Le to uncertake this examination at this time.
2
Le:CESnow:lml

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.98)
F2C101
Office MemOrandum · UNITED STATES GOVERNMENT
Dishilnid 0 FBC 27s/47.
TO
: JA - Mr. Borton
DATB: January 2, 1947.
FROM
Le - C. E. Snow
SUBJECT:
Constitutionality of the Laws Implementing the Japanese
Constitution.
2-3146
1. You have asked Lé for an opinion on the "constitution-
ality" of the Laws Implementing the Japanese Constitution,--
in particular of the Imperial House Law (FEC-lOl) and the Law
for the Election of the House of Councillors (FEC-lOl/1).
2. For the purpose of this opinion it is understood
thet you intend to raise no question under the present
Constitution of Japan (February 11, 1889) but that you refer
only to the New Constitution adopted by the Japanese Diet on
November 3, 1946. So far as the Constitution of 1889 is
concerned, the New Constitution itself and the Implementing
Laws stand on the same footing. Both were enacted by the
National Diet, who declare themselves to be the duly elected
representatives of the Japanese people. If the Diet has the
power, within the present Constitution, to enact a new
Constitution, a fortiori it has the same power to enact the
implementing legislation without which the New Constitution
cannot be made to operate.
9
3. The New Constitution, by express provision, looks to
the passage of implementing legislation. Art. 2 provides
that the Imperial Throne shall be succeeded to "in accordance
with the Imperial House Law passed by the Diet". Art. 43
provides that the number of the members of each House "shall
be fixed by law". Art. 44 provides that the qualifications
of members of both Houses and their electors "shall be fixed
by law"; and Art. 47, that electoral districts, methods of
voting, and other matters pertaining to the method of election
of members of both Houses "shall be fixed by law". Until
the requisite laws referred to by these Articles have been
passed, no new Diet, which is described as "the highest organ
of State power" and the "sole law-meking organ of the State"
can be even elected. Consequently, it is provided, in Art.
100, that the enactment of laws necessary for the enforcement
of the Constitution; the election of members of the House of
Gounillors and the procedure for the convocation of the
Diot and other prep&ratory procedures", may be executed
Cat
before

=== Page 309 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
before the effective date of the enforcement of the New
Constitution. The implicetion is clear that such laws can
be pessed by the present Diet.
4. In this connection &ttention is invited to the
provision in Art. 103 that members of the House of
Representetives and other public officials in office on
the effective date of the Constitution shall not forfeit
their positions on such effective date, nor until their
successors are elected "unless otherwise specified by
lew". This provision clearly looks to a continuation of
the powers of the present government to the effective date
of the New Constitution with & carry-over to provide
opportunity for elections under the New Constitution.
5. Art. 98 of the New Constitution provides that the
Constitution "shall be the supreme law of the State", and
that no public law contrary to the provisions thereof
"shall have legal force or validity". Art. 81 designates
the Supreme Court as the court of lest resort with power
to determine the constitutionality of any law, and
Art. 99 enjoins on Emperor, Diet and Judges the obligation
to uphold the Constitution. Consecuently, sny implementing
legislstion designed to carry over the effective date of
the Constitution must be examined to make sure that 1ts
provisions are not contrary to the provisions of the
Constitution. It is understood that you do not desire Le
to undertake this examinction at this time.

Mr. Secretary
SUBJECT: Publication of FEC Policy Decision requiring
Review of New Japanese Constitution by Japanese
894.011/2-1947
Diet and FEC.
1. When the new Japanese Constitution came before the FEC, all members
approved the Constitution, but on condition that it would be reviewed by the
Diet and by the FEC not sooner than one year or later than two years after it
to
became effective. The United States Government concurred in the Decision, th
the reservation that the FEC would consider at a subsequent meeting the timeS
and manner of publication of the Decision.
2. Members of the FEC have urged that the Decision be published in Japan.
The U. S. Government agreed to request SCAP formally to advise the Japanese
Government of the contents of the FEC Decision without requiring that it be
published. General MacArthur has complied with this request.
3. General MacArthur has opposed publication of the Docision in Japan
for reasons set forth in radios C-66818 dated 1 November 1946 and C-67341 dated
18 November 1946 (TAB A). He has asked that these radios be brought to your
personal attention. General MacArthur fears that publication in Japan would
have disastrous consequences in that it would undermine public confidence in
the Constitution and would be construed as a threat of Allied intervention.
4. In January 1947, an AP dispatch from Tokyo discussed the FEC Decision
in garbled form. Members of the FEC thereupon again urged that the text of the
Decision be published in Japan. However, General MacArthur still opposes this
course(Radio CX 69727, dated February 4, 1947 - Tab B). General MacArthur states
that while he has no objection to publication of the Policy Decision outside
of Japan, his objection to its publication in Japan still holds as previously
expressed.

and not agree to an FEC Decision requiring General MacArthur to publish the
D

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
0
AUSTRALIAN EMBASSY
-P
Y
Washington, D. C.
7 February, 1947
Major General Frank R. McCoy,
United States Representative,
Far Eastern Commission,
2516 Massachusetts Avenue,
Washington, D. C.
Dear General McCoy,
It is reported in the Washington "Star" today that General MaaArthur
has taken action to secure a new Diet election in Japan as soon as prae-
ticable after the present sessions of the Diet.
This would seem to make it essential that the Japanese people should
be informed as soon as possible of the Far Fastern Commission's decision
that the new Japanese constitution should be reviewed by the Diet not
sooner than one year and not later than two years after it goes into ef-
fect. In casting their votes, the Japanese people should know that they
are voting for a Diet which may have the task of revising the constitu-
tion. They should also know this sufficiently in advance for the matter
to be discussed during the election campaign.
You will recall that in the past General MacArthur has laid great
stress on the fact that the new constitution was adopted by & Diet
elected in a campaign where the constitution was an election issue. For
example, in his public statement on 21 Jume, 1946 (FFC 031/14), the
Supreme Commander said: *...the last election, which qualified the
members of this diet, was held with the government draft constitution
squarely before the people and under the paramount consideration that
those elected would be charged with the duty of acting thereon. Few
elections in modern times could be regarded as more truly democratic,
reliable and expressive of the free will of the people. As a consequence
the diet which emerged therefrom is fully representative and qualified to
express the will of the people on this issue." General MacArthur made a
similar point in his reply to the Far Eastern Commission, which was circu-
1ated on 4 June, 1946, as FFC-031/11.
It would, therefore, seem desirable that in the coming elections the
Japanese people should be equally aware of the fact that they may be
voting for the body which will revise the constitution.
I hope that these views will be of some assistance to you in your
present consideration of the publication of the Commission's decision on
the review of the constitution.
Yours sincerely,
/s/ J. Plimsoll
J. Plimsoll
Australian Delegation
Far Eastern Commission

In pronouncing upon the report of the Cabinet's resimation, the
Headquarters' spokeman left undecided the questiom whether or mot the
Prime Minister, whose appointment as well as the attestatiem thereof is
made by the Emperor, may not alone report his own resignation to the
Throne. In the light of Article 6 of the Constitution, which mpowers
the Emperor to appoint the Prime Minister, and of Article 68, which in
turn powers the latter both to appoint and to remove Ministers of
State, such a separate and distinct act of resignation would not appear
out of question. Article 70 would then render necessary the subsequent
resignation of the remainder of the Cabinet en masse. The possibility,
however, of the Prime Minister's standing apart from the rest of the
Cabinet in this respect would seem to have been rejected by Japanese
officialdom itself in the present case. The special relation of the
Prime Minister to the Emperor through the appointing power does not
appear, therefore, to entail any special consequence with regard to
procedure of resignation, and it may be assumed that a report by the
Prime Minister to the Throne of his own resignation would have been met
with the same disapproval as has been shown for the report of the entire
Cabinet's resignation.
It was also disclosed by the spokesman to an officer of this Mission
that the concern of Government Section over this matter had been en-
gendered by information which, in that Section's view, indicates that
pre-war, nationalistic elements in and around the Government are striving
to re-establish the Emperor in his pre-war position of authority.
Notwithstanding this consideration and the apparent intention of the
spokesman's pronouncement, that Japanese constitutional practice shall
eschew reporting a cabinet's resignation to the Throme, we are unable to
comprehend how a constitutional interpretation rendered in this manner
could have legal validity other than that which might tmporarily be
conferred by the authority of the Occupying power. We furthermore believe
that this action constitutes a dangerous precedent for interpretation of
the meaning of the Constitution by fiat in the post-Occupation period,
and that it may give rise among the Japanese to an undesirable belief
that their organic law means what "higher authority" wishes it to mean,
thereby undermining the entire conc ept of constitutional and democratic
government.
Respectfully yours,

MISUSE OF EMPEROR SYSTEM BY BUREAUCRATS IS FLAYED
SCAP Spokesman Urges Amendment of Art. 7 of Con-
stitution to Prevent Abuses
A SCAP Government Section spokesman Tuesday charged "Imperialist-
minded" Japanese bureascrats are heading toward usurpstion ef the
sovereignty of the people through attmpts to return the Emperor
system to its old status.
Guy J. Swope at a press conference bitterly condemmed bareameresy
which he said tried to use the Eaperor during the recent Cabinet srisis
beyond his true position as a ceremonial "symbol of the State and ef
the unity of the people" under the mew Constitution.
He recommended amendment of Article 7 of the Constitution te
prevent misuse of the Emperor system by a bureaucracy which tortured
the "attestation" functien ascribed to the Emperor by permitting the
reporting to the throne of the Cabinet resignation--not a world sem-
cerning which can be found in the Constitution.
He said: "The events of the last fortnight indicate a tendemey to
mbroil and abuse the imperial institution in the same mammer whick
brought Japan to the disaster of war and defeat. For some curieus
reason reports were made to the Eaperor on matters which the Throse,
under the Constitution, has no concern whatsoever.
Hr. Swope mphasised that the Katayame Cabinet had no business to
report to the Emperor before resigning.
He said: "The Constitution provides for attestatiom of appeistmemt
and dismissal of ministers of state. Attestation consists is the mere
authentisation of the fact...is purely ceremonial and symbelical...has
no political consequence.
"If the word 'attestation' can be tortured by a bureamcreey steeped
in the tradition of monarchic rule as to permit the reporting to the
Throne of the resignation of a Cabinet..ethem it is pessible fer sech
Imperialist-minded bureaucrats eventually to usurp the sovereigmty of
the people which is the fountainhead of the Constitutiom."

=== Page 334 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Despatch No. 249 dated

(COPY)
STATEMENT BY MR. WILLIAM J. SEBALD, DEPUTY POR
THE SUPREME COMMANDER, MEMBER FOR THE UNITED
STATES AND CHAIRMAN. ALLIED COUNCIL FOR JAPAN.
In looking back over the year which has passed since the enforee-
ment of Japan's new Constitution, it is at once apparent that the
Japanese people are keenly aware of the priceless possession which
is in their hands. Personal freedom, equality of opportunity, the
guarantees of freedom of speech, of religion, and of thought, as
well as respect for the fundamental human rights, are principles for
which other peoples, too, have striven throughout the centuries. I
can only hope that in the future which lies ahead, the Japanese people
wdll tenaciously maintain these principles and uphold and preserve
the deep and rich philosophy which forms the basis for their Constitution.
I would therefore say to the Japanese people: be ever on the alert
for false leaders who, in the mme of your Constitution--your most
precious asset--would seek to destroy the freedom upon wich your
democracy rests.

suBJEct: Australian Proposal To Send Draft Coneultative Message to SoAp
Regarding Review of Japanese Constitution
PArticipAnts: Mr. H. W. Bullock, Second Secretary, Australian Embassy
Mr. J. M. Allison, Deputy Director, FE

89
Mr. Bullock, member of the Australian Delegation to the Far
Eastern Commission, called on Mr. Allison at 4:30 p.m. on November 10,
l948, in order to discuss a pro osed draft consultative message to
SCAP regarding the review of the Japanese Constitution, which he was
planning to submit to the FEC in the near future.
xK
Mr. Allison stated to Mr. Bullock that he considered it a
possibility that the FEC might decide not to review the Constitution.
-1048
He pointed out that a message to ScAp at this time seeking his views
on the subject seemed premature. He noted that, according to the
policy decision passed by the FEC, only the Japanese people and the
FEC were authorized to review the Constitution, and not SCAP. He
also pointed out that a message requesting Scap to state his vtewe
C
S
on a subject, the creation and development of which he was largely
responsible for, would place SCAp in an embarrassing position.He

added that it would be wise to delay a consultative message to ScAp

of the Constitution should be considered in terms of its effect
on the Japanese people and that for this reason changes should
be kept at a minimum.

However, in view of the strong objections to the
Constitution recorded in FEC discussions of two years ago,
it is not likely that the FEO will?agree to review the
Constitution. It is more than likely that definite pro-
posals will be made to change the document.
Should this be the case, it 1s evident that para-
graph 12 of NSC 13 precludes the Us Government from either
taking positive action in the FEC to amend the dooument
or accepting changes proposed by other governments unless
such changes are supported by the Japanese people. Two
factors stand out. First, the Japanese Constitution 1s
an American-inspired document based on the western poli-
tical philosophy. Secondly, whatever may be our views
regarding this partioular dooument or the philosophy on
which it is based, there 1s little doubt that this doou-
ment represents ideas xtraneous to Japanse oulture,
political heritage and traditional practice. The present
Japanese Constitution, unless modified to accord with
fundamental Japanese ideas, will have as little success
as the Weimar Constitution of the German Republio.
It would merely exaggerate the situation for the FEO
to change the Constitution so as to make it conform in
all aspects with the constitutions of the politioally
sophisticated western democracles. Rather it is now up
to the Japanes& to work with the dooument, absorb as much
of it as they can, modify it to meet their needs and amend
it on their own according to provisions contained in the
Constitution. Should a movement arise among the Japanese
people to change the Constitution at this time, I see no
reason why the Us Government should object prov1ded it
does not endanger the overall objectives of the occupation
in Japan. The Japanese, however, have shown little interest
to date in the general problem of constitutional amendment
and there appears to be considerable feeling that any
amendment, other than terminological clarification, would
be premature at this point. In August and September,
there were indications that a Diet committee was to be
established to study the constitution, but to date the
actual establishment of the committee has not been conrirmed.
Should certain members of the FEC propose that a
referendum be held at this time, it 1s suggested that, des
pite the fact that NSC 13 does not preclude such action,
the Us oppose such action on the ground that there is
adequate provision in the Constitution for amendment. As
tated

the document, however emall, would merely supply ammunition
to those who oppose the American potition in the occupation
of Japan.

A meeting was held in  r. Claxton's office, December 1, 1948 to discuss
the uS position to be taken in FEC with regard to review of the Japanese Con-
stitution. r. Bishop submitted for FE and NA a recommendation (copy attached)
as to the instructions to be furnished the US Member.
It was agreed that the position set forth in paragraphs 1 and 2 of the
recommendations should be adopted. r. Bisnop was of the opinion that we should
now take the firm position, as indicated in para 3, that we would accept no
recommendations for changes in the Constitution and accept no proposals for
action in FEC beyond those set forth in para 1. General Snow, lr. Claxton and
Ir. Barnett agreed that tis would probably be our final position but thought
that it should not be taken officially at this time and not until we had had
the opportunity to hear what changes or action other countries might prpose.
After considerable iscussion it was agreed that in the unlikely event at
member of FEc made an overvhelmingly convincing case for a change in the Co
stitution or for action by FEC the US would of course consider such case. 
was arreed therefore that in order not to foreclose tiis possibility and to
avoid the appearance of takin an unalterable position in advance of reew
of the matter in the FEC, paragraph 3 of the econmendations could be omitted.
894.01
It was felt that the s position would be adequately described in this respect
by para 1 of the ecommenuations. It was further agreed that the US Member
snould advance argunents as to why revision of the onstitution would be un-
necessary and uncesirable and that the memorandum of instruction should outline
such an argument

CONFIDENTIAL
Recommendations
It is recommended that the US Member be instructed:
1) To adopt a firm position that the FEC should, as incon-
spicuously as possible, and with minimur of debate, review promptly
the Constitution and reach a determination that that Constitution
meets all requirements within the purview of the FEC without any
further investigation or action (such as requiring a referendum or
any other procedure to determine Japanese public opinion) on the
part of the FEC.
2) To state that the US Government considers that it is the
responsibility of the Japanese to amend the Constitution if and when
amendment is desiratle or necessary.
3) To accept no recommendations proposed by the FEC for changes
in the Constitution and to accept no proposals for any action by the
FEC beyond that set fortn in paragraph 1.

CONSTITUTION CF JAPAN
Wo, the Japenese peoplo, acting through our duly elected representa-
tives in the National Diet, determined that we shall secure for ourselves
and our posterity tha fruits of peaceful cooperation with all nations and
the blosoingo of liberty throughout this land, and resolved that nover
again shall we be visited with the horrors of war through the action
of gcverrment, do proclain the sovereignty of the poople's will and
do ordain end establish this Constitution, fcunded upon the universal
principle that government is a sacred trust the authority for which
is derived from tho pecple, the powers of which aro cxercised by the
represontatives of the poople, and the bonefits of which are enjoyod
by the people; and we reject and revoke all conetitutions, laws,
ordinances, and rescripts in conflict herewith.
Desiring poaee for all time and fully conscious of the high ideale
controlling human relationehip now stirring mankind, wo have detorminod
to rely fer our security and survival upon the justico and good faith
of tho poace-loving pooples of the world. We desiro to occupy an
honored place in an international society dosigned and dodicated to the
proservation of peace, and the banishment of tyranny and slavery.
oppression and intolerancc, for all time fron the earth. We recognize
and acknowledgc that all peoples havo the right to live in pcace, frea
from fear and want.
We hold thet no puoplc is rcsponsible to itself alone, but that
lawe of political morality are universal; and that obedience to such
laws is inoumbent upon all peoplos who would sustain their cwn
sovereignty and justify their sovercign relationship with othor porploa
To thoso high principles and purposes wo. the Japaneso Peoplo,
pledge our natioual honor, dotorminod will and full resources.

guaranteed to tho people of Japan result fron the age-old struggle of man

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
DRAFT OF MESSAGE TO CENERAL MaCARTHUR
The Far Eastern Commission has begun a review of the Japane
Constitution, as provided in FEC-031/41. The Commission appreciates
that the initiative in any amendment of the Japanese Constitution
belongs to the Japanese and desires to proceed in its review in the
light of the development of Japanese opinion on the Constitution.
It is un erstood that the Japanese Diet has set up an organization
for the purpose of initiating a review. The Commission would appreciate
information as to the progress of the Japanese review and any information
as to how the Constitution has operated in practice which the Supreme
Commander believes may be of assistance to the Commission in its review,
together with any comments the Supreme Commander may desire to make.

In view of the developments that might follow were General
Snow's views to be accepted, I consider it politically advisable
that the US take the position that Japanese Diet has fulfilled
the intent of the FEC policy decision. Any pressure exerted by
the FEC or SCAP to force the Diet to take further action on the
Constitution in view of its obvious disinclination at this time
could have no other effect than to introduce and element of
political instability into a situation already beset with diffi-
cult economic problems.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Files
Central
TELEPHONE
Memorandum of/Conversation
DATE: Fobruary 16, 1949
Review of Japanese Constitution.
SUBJECT:
Mr. Bullock - Australian Embassy
PARTICIPANTS:
8
Mrs. Dunning - NA
COPIES TO:
Mr. Bishop
Mr. Bullock called this morning and informed me that 'He how
had instructions from his Government as to the course he was to
pursue in the FEC with reference to this subject. His Govern-
ment generally agreed with the view expressed by SCAP in his
reply to the FEC consultative message that Allied intervention
to effect changes in the Japanese Constitution at this time was
CS/W
inadvisable. However, his Government took the view that the
Japanese should be encouraged to take an active interest in
their own constitution and that to date the Japanese Diet had
not fulfilled the provision of the FEC policy decision that
"... not sooner than one year and not later than two years
after it goes into effect, the situation with respect to the
new constitution should be reviewed by the Diet." Mr. Bullock
felt that SCAP should encourage the Japanese Diet Committee to
meet and consider this matter. I stated that it was my personal
view that the Japanese Diet had fulfilled such provision.
/Cognizant of (1) the inability of FE and L to reach agreement
on this question several weeks ago and (2) the letter sent by
Mr. Allison to Mr. Sebald concerning this very problem, I thought
there would be no objection to my making this statement on a
purely personal basis. You may be interested in reading a memo
894.011
that I drafted on this very problem on January 28, 1949. In
spite of L's objections and in view of subsequent developments,
17
I feel that an active advocation of the position therein set
forth would have had the effect of curbing this protracted
discussion in the committee
—16
So far as the FEC is concerned, Mr. Bullock thought that
the committee should discuss the views of the member governments
on

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
on various provisions of the Constitution. While he did not
contemplate that the FEC would pass any formal policy decision
on this subject he thought that the views expressed might be
sent informally to SCAP for his guidance. I indicated that the
minutes of meetings were always sent to SCAP.
He furthermore stated that he was considoring proposing
that the FEC send out another consultative message to SCAP
thanking him for his prompt reply, indicating its general agree-
ment with his views on the Constitution, informing him that the
FEC plans to discuss various aspects of the Constitution and
that the summary of these discussions would be sent informally
for his guidance, and suggesting to SCAp that the Diet Commit-
tee meet and review the situation with respect to the Constitu-
tion. I expressed a personal lack of enthusiasm for any
further consultative messages as being completely unnecessary.
Mr. Bullock concluded by stating that he hoped a meeting
of the Constitution Committee could be arranged for early next
week so that the FEC might complete their review as soon as
possible. I expressed complete agreement with the latter objec-
tive. He added as a footnote that he had the agreement of
several other delegations in his proposed suggestions.
In view of this conversation it is recommended that
1) the Department adopt the firm and final position that
the Japanese Diet has fully complied with the provision in the
FEC policy decision to review the situation with respect to the
Constitution;
2) should L and O continue to object to the FE position,
which was made manifestly clear at the time the question was
under discussion, and perhaps cite Mr. Allison's letter to
Mr. Sebald as recognition of a weak legal position, FE should
take the position that this letter was merely an attempt to
settle harmoniously an intra-Departmental controversy and that
since no reply has yet been received and in view of the fact
that a US position is urgently required, political considera-
tions must outweigh all others.
